It must awaken in him vanity, self-consciousness and self-glorification, which struggle fearfully with the truth-seeking, truth-loving temper that led him to say, "I did not choose this course for myself; I did not make this discovery. A mightier wisdom has guided me and shown me what I could never have found." The histories of such struggles are not written, or written very imperfectly, even in the autobiographies and secret confessions of great teachers. Another day may reveal them, with all the strange contradictions that provoked our harsh judgments and should have drawn our pity and sorrow. The outward results are better known and often tragic. Of the Pythagoreans we have only indistinct glimpses, but enough, with the experience we have since gained, to show how zealous the society must have been to bring others into its light, yet how proud in boasting of an exclusive hold on it; how resolved not to separate their moral practices from their outward doctrines, yet how apt, in the heat of propagandism, to drop all inconvenient austerity and to tolerate and use the corruptions they undertook to remove; how their first aim will have been to use their society to make all society deeper in its foundations and truer in its acts, yet how at last they may have come to think it had no deeper foundation than the Pythagorean rule, and that false and dishonest means might be legitimate to establish that rule. Some traditions represent the founder as advancing the ambitions of his order and sharing in its downfall. We cannot test their accuracy. If true, they need not make us doubt the sincerity of his purpose or the real worth of the principle he testified to. The failure of a noble scheme may vindicate its planner's inner conviction better than success would. If Pythagoras believed that human society had a mysterious, divine ground, and that every true philosopher and reformer lives to convince society of it, it was fitting that he and his order should perish when they began to fancy they could build society by their devices, on their wisdom. His name remained sacred and venerable for Greece, though none could say in words what it had done for them. Those who spoke of Pythagorean doctrines in earlier times meant the doctrine of Philolaus, Lysis, Eurytus and Archytas, men who knew nothing of the Italian master, never shared his discipline, and were raised amid ordinary Greek influences. Those who spoke of Pythagoras himself in neo-Platonic times meant a wonder-worker they had created by mixing Christian and pagan records, to convince the world that the Christian church was a plagiarism. But Plato and Aristotle kept a reverence for the original master's name that they never gave to the school where his opinions were dried for use and export. Iamblichus and Porphyry were unconsciously testifying that the best and wisest teachers of ancient Greece had been led, by all their studies of nature and man, and, as they rightly judged, by some guide higher than either, to seek a brotherhood that did not rest on human wisdom; that they tried to create one, and failed.

#### SECTION V. XENOPHANES—PARMENIDES—ZENO.

1\. I have observed before that most of the Greek sages were poets. Verse seemed the natural language for thoughts meant as oracles, for the experienced ear and puzzling to the crowd. Once the wise man became the philosopher, he used verse less often. Empedocles had a rich, poetic mind, but Anaxagoras, Anaximander and Anaximenes probably never left ordinary speech. Yet whichever medium they used, a suspicion began to rise in them: the poet, insofar as he was a Maker, was the philosopher's antagonist. Homer held the sympathies of the Greeks as the most profound student never did. Why? Was his influence healthy? Were his creations not obstacles to the investigator, since he had assumed the very result the inquirers were still pursuing?

2\. This sense of opposition reached its height in *Xenophanes*. Like Pythagoras he was an Ionian by birth and became an Italian colonist. He felt bitterly the luxury of his own city, Colophon, and was disgusted by the manners and love of amusement among the Greeks of Asia and the islands. He must have gone to Italy a discontented man, discontented probably with the Ionian investigations as well as with their political life. Pythagoras's example, or his own reflection, will have taught him that what he wanted was not an element or ἀρχή. But he seems to have had little interest in geometry or arithmetic, and music did not impress him as it did Pythagoras. Pythagoras's thoughts on the soul and its migrations took no hold of him. In one fragment he ridicules a sage who would not let a dog be beaten, because he recognised in its growl the voice of an old friend.

3\. Nor does he seem to have wanted to found or join any political association. He may have heard of the scattering of the Pythagorean society and turned away from such experiments. But his genius did not lead that way anyway. Social unity was not his problem. His problem concerned unity in quite a different sense. What are the gods of the Homeric poems? Does any reality correspond to them? Are they not formed in the poet's brain and clothed by him in sensible forms and images? Is it to sensible forms and images that our minds do homage? Pythagoras had approached this last question from a different side and in a different spirit. He had recognised a Being near to man, to be adored in silence and awe. That Being had little in common with the gods of his countrymen, yet he never denied that they were owed homage. His secret teaching to his disciples cannot have been that the homage was merely a concession to the vulgar; it must have been an effort to make it more sincere and significant. Pythagoras felt he had no substitute for the personal objects he had been taught to revere. He felt that some living being, not an abstraction and not a creation of his own mind, must sustain his polity and every human one.

4\. In Xenophanes all these checks on free inquiry into his country's faith were missing. The naked question came before him: "What does my soul affirm about God; what conceptions can it or can it not form of Him?" His verses, written in various styles and measures, were his answers. As a poet-philosopher he criticises the poets, to show that they have been making the beings they bow down to. The Homeric gods all have human shapes because men made them in their own likeness; if an ox made a god, would he not give him horns and hoofs? How can any conception of God be formed? What do your finite and infinite mean, if both are terms of your own mind? How can you make Him out of them?

5\. This may sound like scepticism, but in Xenophanes it was not. He did not argue that because the senses cannot tell us of God, and we cannot measure Him by our conceptions, He therefore is not. He said the reverse: "My senses do not tell me what is; they tell me only of appearances. My conceptions do not measure what is; it lies deeper." So he did not deny the being of that which the Homeric pictures failed to resemble. His disapproval of them came from his wish to assert a real ground of things, independent of man's conclusions or conceptions, which he affirms to be but does not make.

This was a wonderful process of thought, and our later history must unfold its results. Xenophanes was no atheist but an earnest theist. He asserted *a* Being. Asked "*what* Being?", he would have owned he could not say; he could only say what that Being was not. He approached the border of negation manfully and reverently, and so did not cross it. He pointed out a void he could not fill, and for that alone we should be grateful. But he also saw the way to a deep and radical truth.

6\. A healthier thinker than Xenophanes, though one in whom it is harder to take the same interest, took up his inquiry: *Parmenides*. The question about God that had so occupied Xenophanes does not seem to have agitated him nearly as much. His mind rested on Xenophanes's principle that what the senses present are appearances, and that only what the mind affirms without the senses actually *is*. What, then, does the mind affirm? Xenophanes had said "God." Parmenides said "Unity," or "The One." My senses tell me thousands of things, yet has not every thinking and feeling man groped after some one root and ground of them all? This is what man wants, and it is affirmed to be by that within him which fights against apparitions and phantoms. Plurality is one such apparition, deceitful and transitory; only unity is permanent and eternal. Such a belief could not dawn on a Greek philosopher without deep wonder. He had seen Ionians in succession question all nature for this unity. He had seen Pythagoras evoke it from the relations of number and actually build a human society to illustrate it. Now unity declared itself a condition of the human mind itself; it had been sought in all other things, when it really dwelt only with the mind. A child's confused look at a new world is a faint emblem of the surprise of an earnest seeker on whom this thought has just burst. Yet he cannot doubt it is true. It makes intelligible so much that had perplexed him, and accounts so well for what other men have thought. But what follows? Faith in the things about us becomes impossible; we live in a shadow world and behold nothing. The distinctions of things, the apparent multitude of objects, do not exist and speak only to our fancy. Only the unity the mind beholds and demands has substance.

7\. Anyone can see how this doctrine exposed Parmenides to the jests of witty men, who grow on the surface of every land, and Greece and her colonies produced their share. These wits no doubt thought they were setting self-evident facts against mere dreams. But Parmenides had a friend and disciple unwilling to leave them undisturbed in this opinion. *Zeno* of Elea was convinced that the doctrine experience seemed so irresistibly to establish was not only false but absurd, and he boldly undertook to make the absurdity palpable to the popular mind. In a series of arguments, some still preserved, he tried to show that space cannot exist; that we cannot suppose a plurality of objects without giving them self-destructive qualities; that if real existences are many, their number must be both finite and infinite; and lastly that the notion of movement involves a contradiction. Readers may not care for these early specimens of Greek subtlety and may denounce them as a word-conjurer's tricks. Zeno deserves no such name. He was brave in both action and speculation, and we owe him a great practical discovery. He holds an especially important position as a thinker, and it will help our later studies of Greek philosophy to understand it.

8\. Every philosophy needs an instrument or organ to make itself intelligible. For the Ionic philosopher it was some external object; for the Pythagorean, lines and numbers. What was the Eleatic's instrument? He seems to have risen into a region of such pure metaphysic, rejecting every common and sensible analogy, that at first one cannot see how he could impart his doctrine or suggest any method for pursuing it. Xenophanes seems to have felt this difficulty, and Parmenides somewhat. Precious as their hints are, it seems likely they would have had to stop where they were and leave no successors. But Zeno solved the puzzle. He found that *words* stand to this philosophy as sensible objects and numbers stand to the other two. Our language must embody the law and principle of our own mental workings, and that was exactly what the Eleatics were dealing with.

9\. Zeno was only imperfectly conscious of this truth, but he acted on it, with useful fruit for him and for us. There was no falseness in his use of words. He felt that they affirm and embody truths, and he used them to elucidate truths. In this he was as honest as the wits who tried to confute Parmenides and his kind by appeal to experience. For they too profit from our belief in words. They awaken our consciousness that the words we speak carry the impress and image of the external world, and they rest on this against philosophers who set the evidence of the external world at nought. Zeno awakens our consciousness that the words we utter express something with no counterpart in the external world, and he rests on this against those who set at nought the witness of their own minds. Both appeals are fair and carry conviction. But one merely convinces us of a fact we already took for granted; the other forces us to perceive truths lying very near our inmost being, yet almost entirely hidden from us.

10\. But the practical discovery that came directly from this search for an instrument for the Parmenidean philosophy is what most obliges us to admire and thank Zeno. According to the best historical evidence, we owe mathematical science to the East, and it fits the calm, contemplative yet sensual character of the Orientals that this was their contribution. But *Logic*, the science that declares not the conditions of external things but the conditions under which we ourselves speak and judge, was purely a Greek invention. No other people had the subtlety to conceive such a science, still less to find its province and work. Logic in its formal, narrow sense is thought the antagonist of poetry, yet only a most imaginative and poetical nation could have discovered its meaning and necessity and given it its statue-like perfection in Greek hands. Zeno is believed, on the best grounds, to be the inventor of logic. He first saw clearly that the mind has a distinct law governing its own affirmations, and so was first stirred to ask what that law is. How much we owe him we shall understand better as we advance. My main object here has been to point out the connection between this achievement and the Eleatic philosophy, which Zeno ably defended; to indicate the influence of that philosophy on the Greek mind; to show its important place in the history of human inquiry; and to excite interest in the later development of a doctrine we have seen in its first germ. In so rapid a sketch we can only notice what seems the living and central peculiarity of each thinker. But the real germinant principle is hard to find amid the mass of notions and opinions around it. An historian will distrust his own sagacity in detecting it, and will rejoice to find it anywhere in its rude, primitive shape. However little value it has in that state, he thinks it unwise to wait until the animalcule has become a perfect insect, or the insect has died, before examining it. This is my apology for treating the ante-Socratic schools briefly, yet at a length that may seem out of proportion to the time we can give their successors. We wish not to furnish readers with a history but to put them in a right method of procuring one for themselves, and I am convinced that their understanding of the palmy period between Socrates and Aristotle, and of the age of senility that followed, will be clear or confused exactly in proportion as they study or pass over its infancy. Let them not hope to understand Plato, Aristotle, or even Epicurus, Zeno of Cittium and Carneades, if they begin by despising Heraclitus, Pythagoras and Parmenides. We might go further and say we would greatly doubt the claim of anyone to know Hobbes, Locke, Leibnitz or Kant who could find nothing but confusion and barrenness in these early inquiries.

### DIVISION II.—GREEK PHILOSOPHY FROM SOCRATES TO ARISTOTLE.

#### SECTION I. ATHENS IN THE TIME OF SOCRATES.—THE SOPHISTS.

1\. No one doubts that Greece was the country of wisdom and wise men, or that Athens was its capital. There Wisdom was worshipped with all her rites, and wise men had an honour given them nowhere else. The name of the city shaped what its citizens did. Even the meanest of them felt that the Goddess of Wisdom protected him and had given him some endowment.

2\. Nobody disputes either that the age of Pericles was when Athens reached the glory she had always aimed at, and her sons' powers came to manhood. Pericles thoroughly understood the people he ruled. The funeral oration Thucydides gives him may not have been delivered in just this form, but it expresses the exact perception of the Athenian mind which the historian knew Pericles had. If the speech is not a report, it still rightly shows Pericles's settled purpose: not to restrain the tendencies of this character but to give them full play. He would let no Spartan moroseness interfere with Athenian freedom. Whatever corrections it needed, he believed, must come from itself. To bring in the maxims and habits of another tribe would destroy Athenian energy without giving it the Dorian self-restraint.

3\. Right or wrong, this calculation had memorable effects. We think first of the work of sculptors and poets. The sense of beauty and symmetry in the human form that awoke then, and the images of the gods it produced, have been admired and have shaped later generations. But they belong with the processions and temples they were made for. The great tragedies, too, should be pictured as performed at festivals, competing for a popular prize, before delighted or critical crowds. Those that have lasted more than 2,000 years show how deeply some Athenians thought about the destiny of man. But they stood among many other works probably not felt to be inferior. They were judged by their power to amuse at the moment, by their fit with the demands of very clever but probably very impatient spectators. An author's popularity was measured by how quickly and versatilely he could stir sorrow or mirth, although the man who had thought and felt most would stir the deepest echoes and in the end win the greatest reverence. Aeschylus and Sophocles probably towered over their contemporaries even in the judgment of the many. Still, the qualities we admire in them must largely have been forgotten in the immediate effects they produced. It was for later times to find how much in them no show could exhaust, and that it belonged not to one age or nation but to mankind.

4\. Why, it may be asked, do such reflections suit the age of Pericles more than that of James I.? Were not Ben Jonson and Fletcher thought of mainly as makers of masques for the court, and Shakspeare as an actor at the Globe? That is true. But a student of philosophy, above all of the life of Socrates, has special need to remember how all the great men of Athens whose instruments were words appeared to their fellow-citizens. All were practising some kind of *wisdom*, and addressed it, in the theatre or the agora, to judges who were themselves wise and conscious of wisdom, able to appreciate it and to reward it. The tragedians, with their minds fixed on the heroic ages, kept their wisdom from becoming a creature of the mob's. A comic writer, whose business was with the present, needed very high genius to resist that influence; he would do so partly by choosing the broadest and most conspicuous conceits and affectations of his age to ridicule. But those who used wisdom for entertainment faced the lesser temptation. Those who used it directly to persuade, who spoke in order to lead citizens to deeds, stood in a far more dangerous position for their honesty and could do greater and more widespread mischief. By degrees their kind of power became the measure of all others. The rhetorician was regarded as the man who had found out the effective use of words. Poets, statesmen, thinkers of every class, even the commonest handicraftsmen, would gradually become rhetoricians, and rhetoric would be looked on as the craft of the wise city.

5\. It has commonly been supposed that a class of men, formed in various Greek cities but always counting Athens their headquarters, kept this tendency alive in young Athenians and gave it a dangerous prominence. They are called Sophists; from them the name has taken on its evil sense; and Socrates's main work has been supposed to be to counteract and undermine their influence. Learned men share this impression with the vulgar, the most modern and critical writers with mere narrators. Recently these notions have been attacked with great skill and apparently with great weight of evidence. The existence of a sophistical system has been flatly denied. Those given the common name, it is said, differed from each other in all their doctrines, pursuits and habits of life. No corrupt purpose or immoral character can be charged against them; proof of their respectability can be had from the books of their greatest opponents. They did not make Athenian society fall from the standard of earlier ages, since the history shows no such decline. The word Sophist was not a term of abuse to the Greeks; it was a synonym for the wise man, and impartial writers gave it to poets, to philosophers, and to the supposed antagonist of sophistry himself. Plato and Aristotle chose to use it badly; they had a right to their own definition, but they cannot give us a right to pass an *ex post facto* sentence on their contemporaries. Finally, we may hold that the object of Socrates's life was good without blaming the Sophists whom he or his disciple blames: he aimed at a universal standard of wisdom and truth, while they professed only to teach Athenians how to think, speak and act. These conclusions, if true, must affect our whole later history. So we should consider how far they are borne out by the able arguments and undoubted facts that Mr. Grote has produced.

6\. We accept at once Mr. Grote's definition of the Sophist as Plato's and the true one: "the professor of wisdom; he taught young men to speak, think, and act." We want no other and no worse account of him. If modern artists have put darker shades into their picture, they have done him a service rather than an injury, because their clumsy exaggeration hides the essential ugliness that Mr. Grote's flattering sketch brings out in full relief.

7\. They have, I think, been especially wrong in trying to blacken the age of Pericles, as though it were inherently worse than any earlier age. In many respects it was much better. Not only were the people's intellectual energies more developed, but their great writers showed a moral insight and purpose not found in older times or in their immediate predecessors. Who can deny that Thucydides has a much higher tone than Herodotus? That the *Oedipus Tyrannus* recognises principle much more deeply than the *Odyssey*? That even Aristophanes (though I agree with Mr. Grote in refusing him the dignity of a moral teacher) tacitly admits, in his satires on bad citizens, a standard that poets and audiences in the times of Solon and Pisistratus would not so readily have perceived? The better men had certainly progressed in moral perception, and that progress could not have been found in them unless their contemporaries were *capable* of the same. The Athenians' political training and discipline must have contributed greatly; their experience of society and government led them to practical distinctions that would otherwise have been hidden from them.

8\. Whatever we think of the Athenian democracy, it clearly did this great service to the eminent men who lived under it, and through them to the world. Its corrupt maxims and practices are known to us through the emphatic protests against them preserved in historical reflections and prophecies, in lessons from the past and ridicule of the present. But the protests also show what the Athenians' peculiar temptation was, why they were more prone to it now than before, and what influences most surely fostered it. We have seen that most of the Greek wise men were tyrants (or despots, as Mr. Grote prefers). The natural use of their wisdom was to gain power, to fit them for governing fools. Every Greek was inclined to this opinion, and it grew with his faculties. Democracy gave him an obvious chance to exercise them in this direction. The Peloponnesian war suggested thoughts, latent in the Persian war, of rivalry between Greeks, of Athenian ascendancy, of the difference between forms of government. The passionate impulses of patriotism, with their own dangers, gave way to deliberate schemes and calculations about winning rule and wielding it. There may not have been more evil-doing in one time than the other, but there must have been more consciousness of it, more inward wickedness, and a greater readiness to bring crimes under a theory and defend them by it. This is what the Melian controversy inevitably suggests. Allow what you please for the writer's aristocratic leanings: an experienced and wise man credits his fellow-citizens with a distinct understanding of an evil purpose and its principles such as we meet nowhere else. Yet we would have attributed such an understanding to a people of Athenian wit and subtlety even without his authority.

9\. To give this wit and subtlety full play, all that was needed was for a set of men to appear who started from the same maxim as the wise men in general, but had instruments those men lacked, and who were ready to *teach* the skill that others had *used* in a narrower sphere for their own advantage. The professors I am speaking of fit this description exactly. They had all the respectability Mr. Grote claims for them; many were aged and grave; they were men of uncommon sagacity and penetration. They had studied in different schools, some under Empedocles, some under Zeno. Some gave themselves to physical studies, some to moral, some directly to political. Each had some sort of wisdom and undertook to teach it. Each held out the winning of political power as the prize. That was their common point of agreement, and possibly their only one. Young Athenians wanted to learn how to think, act and speak on all subjects so that they might guide the people as they pleased, and for this they sought a Sophist or Professor.

10\. The Athenians certainly needed to learn to *think*. What was the teaching for that? They had to be told about natural, moral and political subjects by men who had taken pains to learn what Thales, Pythagoras and Parmenides said of them. They had to be told the views of ancient cosmogonists about the world and the gods, and of modern thinkers and allegorists on the same subject. They had to hear of the Heraclitan flux and the Parmenidean One; of how cities were said in former days to be built by the lyre of Apollo, and how they were held together by the skill or legislation of recent despots. They were taught to compare all these views, to see the strength of one and the weakness of another, or to combine and reconcile them. Thus the Sophist taught his pupils to think. But all thinking is for the sake of *action*. These professors are thoroughly practical men; they do not come to draw us away from the world's business, and what would we care for them in Athens if they did? We want to know about men, not stars. We want to defeat Brasidas, or support our party at home against Nicias, or rival Cleon, much more than to know anything of Heraclitus, Parmenides or Zeno. Yet the one learning is the road to the other. Remember what lies between thinking and acting; remember Homer's "winged words," which have always been with Greeks "the great engines of government, the proximate cause of obedience." If we teach you to *speak*, we teach you in the most efficient way to act. These physical, metaphysical and moral theories will furnish you with topics, they will be the tools of your trade, and they will give you a wonderful power to embarrass, confute and overawe an uninstructed opponent. Any of them may serve. Theories of the order and formation of the world, in the skilful hands of Hippias, may make as good a rhetorician as direct moral teaching from Prodicus, or speculations on government, human or divine, from Protagoras. All supply topics; all are instruments of persuasion. And see what power Zeno has put into our hands! Words may mean the most opposite, contradictory things. Would it not be invaluable lore to be taught the secret of this contradiction and how to turn it to account?

11\. In the last paragraph I hinted at the modern meaning of the word Sophist, which Mr. Grote so indignantly rejects. It is certainly not *the* meaning; the one he has put in its place is far more comprehensive and satisfactory. But by the necessity of his calling, the man who taught thinking, acting and speaking would come to see the last as including the other two. He would become a rhetorician and a teacher of rhetoric. For that he must deal with the subtle meanings of words, honestly as Zeno did or treacherously, depending on his aim. If his aim was to sway the mind of a mob, he was at least in considerable danger of leading his pupils to give sophistry the force with which we are most familiar.

*Note.* Since I have admitted the Sophists' general respectability, I need not examine Mr. Grote's arguments for it in each case. But one statement he relies on needs a short notice. He thinks Prodicus can be shown by more than negative evidence to have been a highly moral teacher, because the story of the Choice of Hercules, as we usually read it, names him as its author. What better proof, he asks, that Prodicus urged on Athenian youth a severe, even ascetic, self-restraint? I have no wish to dispute the beauty or worth of that fable; it was surely full of instruction for that age, since all ages have found it so. But I submit that its lesson does good or harm according to the reader's aim. If he wants the power to drain marshes and kill noisome beasts, all must bless him for not yielding to the Goddess of Pleasure. If he merely wants to be the strongest of men by resisting the enchantress, it might have been better for the world and for himself if he had yielded. Mr. Grote surely remembers Gibbon's paradox about the clergy: "Their virtues are more dangerous to society than their vices." On Gibbon's presumed hypothesis, that the order divides into those who deny themselves to gain dominion over their fellows and those who give way to animal indulgence, the saying is easily granted. The monk who restrains his appetites to be followed and idolised as a confessor does more harm to others, and is probably more evil in himself, than the sleek abbot given up to hawks and hounds. The principle applies universally. Before we can call Prodicus a useful teacher for telling his pupils how to get the bone and nerve of Hercules, we must know whether he departed from the general rule of his class by not holding out political power as his prize.

12\. I cannot think, then, that accomplished scholars and honest men like Ritter and Brandis are fairly accused of imposing on less-instructed readers when they use a phrase like "Die Sophistik" to express their sense that all the different professors of wisdom in the age of Pericles practised one art. That view does not conflict at all with the fact that the word Sophist may have been applied to a poet, or to anyone who exercised influence through words rather than swords—to Thales, to Pythagoras, to Socrates. Astrology has an undoubted meaning, which most people think a bad one; yet is an astrologer any more than one who studies the stars? Why should the man who studied them with the full aim of finding the laws governing their courses not have been called an astrologer, as much as any of the countless doctors who foretold political events from the stars? These traders in natural knowledge were no more one school or guild than the Athenian professors; they had different maxims, were rivals, were enemies; yet it has been usual to think they had a common work that a common name can denote. And every man who claimed to be an astronomer, not an astrologer, had to make good his claim by the labours of a life and show how he differed from the caster of nativities. In doing so he must put a stigma on a name not necessarily evil before, and gain for himself a name in some sense new. His eventual compensation will be to vindicate the fame of many worthy predecessors who were not distinctly conscious of their own end but honestly sought for light while others boasted that they possessed it and could turn it to account. Till he has finished his task he must bear the same reproach as those he most opposes, and from whom he is trying to deliver his fellows.

13\. Many at this time scorned and ridiculed the young men of Athens for attending one Sophist or another, and for showing the effects of the teaching in their self-conceited words and acts. Aristophanes above all could teach these young men to laugh at themselves, at their own thoughts, speculations and imaginations as well as their teachers'. In doing so he perhaps expected to restore the habits of an older and, as it seemed to him, simpler method.

Neither reason nor evidence allows us to believe that his success matched his zeal or genius. He may have abated some of the nuisances infesting Athens; he may have thinned the race of sycophants, made the vulgar kinds of mob-persuasion less effective, even done something to curb his fellow-citizens' litigious spirit. But he can have done very little to root out the real cancer of the nation's being, which fed on the hearts not of the worst but of the best, noblest and most promising of the Athenian youth. No one could apply a sound remedy to this evil who despised the age he was born into, who saw only the effects of the sophistical poison without understanding its nature and the constitutions it worked on. Only he could hope to reform the young men of Athens who sympathised with them heartily and affectionately; who did not show contempt or indignation for their favourite teachers but was ready to follow them through all their windings and subtleties; who, without for a moment forgetting his purpose of finding his way back to realities, could fearlessly grapple with the most shadowy and impalpable abstractions. Such a man would have sore difficulties, through which only a clear perception of his object could lead him unhurt. His inward conflicts, before he was fit for his task, must be severe. The greatest of his outward ones would perhaps be this: those who were aiming, with very different instruments, to resist the same evils would infallibly misconstrue his purpose. He would inevitably pass with them for a Sophist, perhaps the subtlest and most mischievous of them. Because he sought to make men feel there was no resting-place in any of their theories or opinions, he would be suspected of universal scepticism. Because he led them to feel that they were not without ground to stand on if they would seek it, he would be accused of undermining the ground their forefathers stood on. Because he tried to look through the clouds drawn up from the earth into the serene heaven behind them, it would be fancied that he invoked the clouds' protection and did them homage.

Such a man was Socrates, and this was his fate. Sophists hated him, and he was ridiculed as the worst of them. He treated his country's diseases by a method exactly opposite to Aristophanes's, and so Aristophanes denounced him as their chief promoter. We have now to consider what his method was, how it affected his own age, and what traces it has left of itself for later generations.

#### SECTION II. SOCRATES.

::: {.center}
[born ol. 77, 4; b.c. 468.]{.smallcaps}
:::

1\. [There]{.smallcaps} is little doubt that Socrates was the son of a statuary and a midwife, and that he was born in a small township of Attica. We do not know exactly when he came to Athens. It was probably about the start of the Peloponnesian war, when Pericles was still alive and Anaxagoras was teaching. Socrates attended Anaxagoras's school. He very likely took up his master's studies with real ardour and carried away many valuable hints.

2\. Anaxagoras, as we have seen, stood at the furthest remove from the trading Sophists. For them political life was everything; for him it was nothing. He sought a home in the world of nature, apart from human beings. It was a noble experiment. Young Pericles felt it to be so, though his life's desire was political ascendancy. One might have thought young Socrates, who never showed that desire, would be carried away by it even more. But he could not find a country where Anaxagoras sought one. As he listened to his master's sublime physical speculations, he seems to have asked himself: "What are all these to me? Let atoms be linked by whatever law of affinity, let them whirl at random or be guided by an intelligence; the question still remains, What am I? These speculations do not answer it, yet it must be answered somehow. Any carpenter or shoemaker who could help me solve it would be my benefactor. The profoundest teacher whose thoughts run another way is not the man I want." So he left the school of Anaxagoras. Its quietness was inviting, but if that quietness cost the sacrifice of human feelings and interests, the gossip of the Agora and the bustle of the Piraeus were better.

3\. Like the Sophists, then, Socrates was a man of business and action. His wisdom, if he had any, must be directed to what was passing around him, not to some other sphere. But did he have wisdom he could use for this? Could he teach about things on earth or in the skies? The more he considered, the more he found that he did not possess wisdom; it was exactly what he needed. He could not hand it to disciples to use and traffic with. He had to go in search of it. The distinction between the Sophist, or wise man, and the philosopher had dawned on Pythagoras, perhaps on Thales; for Socrates it became the cardinal distinction. For him to possess or profess wisdom would be utter falsehood. He found he could not lay down theories or maxims even about the commonest things. What he needed was to understand them, and to understand them so that he might understand himself and find out whether he had any ground to stand on or was only floating in the air.

4\. Who could help him in this inquiry? It concerned his very self, which no eye could see and which lay beneath all utterance and thought. Socrates believed there was one near him who took notice of the most secret movements of his mind and will, who reproved, restrained, and warned him. A divine teacher was with him at all times. Though he did not possess wisdom, this teacher could set him on the road to seek it, preserve him from delusions that might turn him off it, and keep his mind fixed on the end for which he was to live and act. Xenophon asks, with plain soldierly honesty, whether the accusers could believe Socrates lied about this. He hints that it would shake his faith in all reality to suppose that so clear-sighted a man, so free from superstition, could be the victim of a wholly false impression, or that such an impression could produce the wholesome effects Xenophon himself had witnessed. We believe that Socrates told no lie about his Demon, and that it was precisely this faith that kept him from lying and made him clear-sighted and free from superstition.

5\. Socrates connected this guide with the mythology of his countrymen, and seems to have interpreted each by the other. He was sure he had such a teacher, so he could acknowledge a teacher of wisdom to Greeks and to men generally. Much that was said about the god of Delphi seemed profane to him, and he turned from it with disgust. But on the whole he believed more, not less, than his countrymen, and shrank from the scepticism and irreverence in which they and their poets indulged. He had no notion of replacing Jupiter or Apollo with a Nous or Intelligence. That would have been strange, since he was not accounting for the existence of the universe but craving a light to show him his own path through it. So he was not a Monotheist in the sense some have claimed; he did not deny that there were various objects of worship, and in many acts of his life he acknowledged them. But he felt there was one teacher, one source of light and wisdom, leading him out of the confusions of sense. He was therefore practically more of a Monotheist than he could have been had he tried to reduce Greek traditions to physical speculations or treated them as mere follies.

6\. Socrates spoke of his Demon as reproving and restraining himself. But because he connected the Demon with his country's gods, he felt that other men also had a director to obey, who could lead them to what he sought. So instead of being a solitary thinker, he had the most intense sense of a vocation to help and instruct others. He could not give them wisdom, but he might put them on the same way of seeking it that he was walking himself.

7\. What charms he used to draw a circle around him may at first seem inexplicable. Most of the Sophists were advanced in age and reputation when he first appeared in Athens. They promised to fit men to be politicians, orators, and generals, and offered plausible proof that they could do it. He promised nothing. He had come, he said, to practise his mother's profession on behalf of those who had thoughts they wished to be delivered of. You could not tell what line he took, whether he was a philologer like Prodicus or a professor of statesmanship like Protagoras; he seemed to be all things by turns and nothing for good. Personal beauty was a great recommendation among the Athenians, yet he had large projecting eyes like a bull's, a flat upturned nose, a protruding stomach, a tattered cloak, and was seldom seen with sandals. Nevertheless the youth of Athens began to flock to him, thinking he had something to teach them, perhaps that he could somehow impart the art of governing better than the regular doctors. Some of the causes we have mentioned as likely to alienate his countrymen may even have contributed to this result. The Athenians liked a humorist, and Socrates showed himself one by his outward carelessness and the whole tone of his talk. He also had a hearty, genial way of interesting himself in whatever interested those he was with, and as little solemn quackery as was ever found in any man. Add that he was thoroughly Greek: in his habits of mind, his taste for society, his wit and argument, his brave unflinching love of his land, and in making freedom (far more than is usually acknowledged) the passion and end of his life. But all this together could not have outweighed his many disadvantages had he not had the real magnet that draws young men's hearts, however reluctant: knowledge of the very thing they want and have been toiling in vain to find.

8\. Political power, as we have seen, was the one prize the Sophists set before themselves and held out to pupils as the reward for their labour on abstract speculations. The roads to it differed, and each teacher believed, and persuaded his disciples, that his was the shortest. But all shared one method: they sought power by means of *words*. Mastery of words was the great art the Athenian youth must cultivate; his own feelings and what he saw daily in his city told him there was a fascination in words that the physical force of an Oriental tyrant could not match. Socrates, when he began earnestly to meditate on his countrymen's condition, seems first to have observed that here, as usually, the tyrants were slaves: those who wished to rule the world by words were in ignominious bondage to words. The wish to break this spell took strong hold of him. But it would have been ineffectual, and would have interfered with the main feeling of his life, had he not been able to connect the study of words with that deep question about his own being. As he reflected, he saw more and more clearly that words are not only the instruments by which we govern others but the means by which we may become acquainted with ourselves. In trying really to understand a word, to find the *bona fide* meaning he himself gave it, he gained more insight into his own ignorance and more real knowledge than by all other studies together. In this work he knew he was honest; he was feeling for a ground and breaking through a thousand tricks and self-deceptions. If he was to deliver his countrymen from the miserable shallowness into which the ambition of wisdom and depth had betrayed them, to lead them out of the multitude of systems above morality into a firm sense that there was a morality, and above all to rescue them from the worship of *power*, this must be his means. He must not stop to weigh the wisdom of this proposition or that. He must not denounce with moral indignation those that struck him as mischievous or outrageous, nor generously concede those that seemed plausible. In every case he must lead his disciples to ask what they actually meant by the words of the propositions they used. He must count no time wasted that they honestly spent on this, and no perplexities or contradictions dangerous that arose from their own minds in the course of it.

9\. This was certainly an irritating, vexatious way to proceed. An opponent who had adopted a proposition and had plenty of arguments for it would be tortured to find himself not fairly met on those arguments, but led back to a question he had taken for granted. He would be forced to account for a word he supposed everyone agreed on, and not allowed to bring any of his own resources into play. It was equally perplexing for a disciple who had come expecting a doctrine to be established or refuted, and the ingenious arguments on both sides to be useful in a popular assembly. He got no decision either way, and learned that he had been talking all his life in a language he did not understand, using words like algebraic characters. Yet somehow the Sophist was taught that he was in the presence of one stronger than himself. He might chafe, fret, and complain of unfair treatment. He could not say his opponent had not beaten him at his own word-fighting, or that all the scepticism he had turned against the common thoughts and feelings of mankind had not come back tenfold on himself. He could not help owning that the man in conflict with him had some other end than the exercise or display of power, and yet had a power before which his own quailed. The disciple, for his part, amid all his bewilderment, went away feeling that he had, perhaps for the first time, actually learned something. He was convinced that if there is nothing better than dominion over other men's minds, there is at least an important and indispensable preliminary to it, unless our own minds are to be the sport of every deceiver.

10\. The infinite humour and vivacity of Socrates must have served him greatly in such dialogues. But his opponents often fancied he was merely indulging his humour when he was in fact following out his principle. His habit of confessing uncertainty or ignorance on any subject, which in their eyes was the chief element of his "irony," was not usually put on. We make no doubt that he often began a discussion without knowing where it would lead, actually hoping, as he said, to learn from the result. He was certain not of a particular conclusion but that his method was sound and would lead each person who followed it to clearness and truth. His discoveries about himself and his fellow-creatures were probably the practical fruits of this method. For instance, by repeated experiments he convinced himself of the immense importance of recollection: the mind without it is at the mercy of accidents, and the mind with it is continually realising its own possessions, receiving them as if bestowed for the first time. Most of his moral discipline rested on this principle. Its purpose and ground was to remove the impediments to recollection and lead the mind from mere sensible images and impressions to an examination of its own treasures. But the principle was saved from any Brahminical tendency by his habitual use of words and sensible images as the means by which a man feels his way into the principles and grounds of his being. A man learns to understand himself by trying to understand common things, what the carpenter does with his wood, the shoemaker with his leather, the mason with his stones, and by really finding out what we mean when we talk of them. So Socrates's withdrawment or recollection did not point to an escape from common life or daily business. It formed the habit a man is to cultivate of seeking out what each thing really is, not merely its shapes, appearances, and accidents, and this is ultimately to fit him to perceive what is deepest and truest. The soul of the doctrine is a faith won by repeated proofs and trials. Man has in him a desire to find out the truth of things, and also an inclination to keep company with mere images of things. As far as the first keeps uppermost and rules the other, he is in his honest, sound position; as far as the lower is uppermost, he becomes a mere shadow-pursuer and shadow-fighter. Socrates did not adopt this as a scheme to supplant another scheme. He stumbled on it as a fact he could no more deny than what his senses showed him, a fact which *was*, however explained, and must be recognised in all our dealings with ourselves or others.

11\. There was one young man in Athens whom Socrates loved intensely. In him the Greek qualities were seen at their highest: creative power, skill with words, personal beauty, fascination of manner. The love Socrates bore him shows how thoroughly he sympathised with the very feelings he feared most and in which he saw all possibilities of evil. If Alcibiades could have learned that there was a right and a wrong, and that man's business is to walk in a line, not devise one, to perceive, not to create, the whole history of Athens might have been different. There must have been critical moments when he confessed to himself that something was more worth seeking than dominion. There must have been moments when the feeling that he too had a guide and monitor within him was stronger than the sense of power and the inclination to wrong that went with it. But Ahriman prevailed over Ormuzd. Alcibiades yielded to the darker power within, which kept tempting him to glorify his own intellect and use his mighty gifts to destroy his country and himself. Then all the skill in word-fighting he had seen in his master became his curse, an instrument of mighty mischief. Once he had parted with the moral purpose Socrates was aiming at, what he received from him became sophistry of the worst kind. It taught him to act more effectively on the maxim that all order and society had been invented by the strongest or cunningest, who could pull down what they had invented.

12\. This, says our recent historian of Greece, was not the sophistical teaching. The Sophists merely meant to fit Athenian young men for civil life; their aim was not so high as that of Socrates, but far from bad. We believe Socrates would have answered: "Either it is *this* aim that I set before myself, or it is a bad aim. All my teaching and influence, if it lacks this aim, is bad teaching and bad influence. My elenchus is in itself nothing better than the logic or rhetoric of any other professor. If it is taken up merely as a more skilful or ingenious art, it will be worse, for its purpose is to lead men to apprehend that which is, by sifting it from its shapes and counterfeits, from that which is not. The Sophists are destroying the heart and soul of my countrymen, because they keep leading them to think that what they want is an *art* to do or to make. What they actually want is a *science*, a means of seeing what they did not make, what lies beneath all our doings and is at the root of ourselves."

13\. From this it will be seen in what sense knowledge seemed to Socrates the basis of morality. Those who suppose he meant to exalt the human faculties and make them the grounds of virtue and truth do not merely mistake but invert his meaning. To destroy the worship of power, especially intellectual power, was the purpose of his life, and he showed this above all in his doctrine of the relation of knowledge to morality. As the outward eye sees certain objects and is good for nothing except as it sees them, so the inward eye perceives certain objects and is good for nothing except as it sees them. The objects are there, and the whole blessing of the man is to behold them. As he beholds them he becomes like them, but they *are*; they are not the variable functions of his mind but the eternal, unchangeable principles and grounds of it. A notice of Socrates is only an occasion for indicating this faith; in speaking of his great disciple we must try to expound it.

14\. Socrates, then, was, as he said himself, a philosopher and nothing else, a philosopher in the strictest sense, who helps us better than anyone to know what philosophy is. He never imagines that his philosophy contains or provides its own object. He is the wisest of men, as the oracle said, because he knows nothing; that is, because he brings nothing with him but acknowledges what presents itself to him. When he speaks of the dignity of the philosopher, he means the dignity of a man who does not exalt himself or put himself in the way of the thing he examines, and who has the simplest, most open eye for receiving light from wherever it comes. That there is a source from which the light comes, and that this light is connected with man, is a principle assumed, however imperfectly developed, in all his words and acts.

15\. How could such a man, it has often been asked, be compelled to drink hemlock? Must not the restored democracy of Athens have been worse and more intolerant than any power ever on earth? Mr. Grote answers, most reasonably I think, that the wonder is how such a man was allowed to go on teaching so long, since no state ever showed so much tolerance for differences of opinion as Athens. I would add this. Had it been possible to regard Socrates merely as an utterer of peculiar opinions, one of the Greek Sophists or professors, he might still have taught with impunity. Anytus and Meletus might have had their own reasons for disliking him, his connexion with Critias or Alcibiades might have roused suspicion, and the comedians' ridicule might have kept up a habitual prejudice, but the tolerance of the Athenian people would have triumphed. He would have been acquitted on the charge of corrupting the youth and on that of introducing new demons. But there has always been, and always will be, a limit to the indulgence of those who regard all opinions as equally possible. A man who positively denies that he is proclaiming an opinion, who speaks of the possibility of *knowing*, the duty of *distinguishing*, and a truth that men do not create and that does not change with our intellect, belongs to a different category from the promulger of opinions, and is not entitled to the same mercy. Tolerant people, on the very ground of their tolerance, feel bound to silence or crush him. What business has he to insult other men's opinions, to tell them there is something it is dangerous not to see, that falsehoods cling to their lives which they ought to cast off? It takes a long time before a thoroughly good-natured man can persuade himself that anyone has reached this height of criminality. All pity will be shown to the man's fanaticism while possible, and he will be treated, in spite of himself, as a sectarian teacher propounding a particular opinion. But if he persists, as Socrates did, in asserting that he is not a Sophist and not the putter-forth of a theory, tolerant men must, and experience shows they will, reluctantly resort to the same racks, dungeons, and poison-cups that bigots use. For it comes to this: if the teacher is right, he must be regarded as a public benefactor, and the city must honour him above all its citizens. When the judges condemned Socrates to death, they asked him, by Athenian custom, what milder sentence he would propose for himself. He answered, "A public support in the Prytaneum." They might be offended at his audacity, but their consciences told them this was the real alternative. Not being prepared to take it, they let the sentence be carried out, and so chose a course far more honourable to Socrates and more instructive to later ages.

16\. We must be careful not to separate the discourse of Socrates after his condemnation from the course of his earlier life. His faith in a future state is often put forward as distinguishing him from his countrymen and the pagan world. But no one refers more often than Socrates himself to the old stories that express this faith: to Aeacus and Rhadamanthus, the functions attributed to them, and the souls they judged. He evidently believed the essence of these stories was true, that they set forth a correspondence between men's condition hereafter and their condition here. As in other cases, he received what earlier men taught but asked himself what it meant and how it concerned him. His countrymen believed that somehow they would be judged hereafter by what they had done here, and that some particle of themselves would suffer a vague punishment or enjoy a vague happiness. He was fixed in the conviction that a man's blessedness consists in knowing that which is, with his soul engaged in the pursuit of that knowledge, and that his misery consists in lacking it and being given up to dreams and unrealities. He hoped he would know hereafter what he had desired to know here. He sought arguments to convince himself that, however the accidents around him changed, he himself would continue, and, freed and purified from the corruptions and restraints he had felt here, would converse with perfect Wisdom and Goodness. Socrates did not tell his disciples that his future life would be separate from his life here; he looked for the continuation and unfolding of that life. He felt that his eyes had been partly opened and would be opened more perfectly, that he would still and always be a seeker after wisdom, but that wisdom would meet and embrace him and ever reveal new treasures, which would awaken ever fresh longings and continually satisfy them. The seeker of wisdom, who passed here for a pursuer of shadows, would grasp substance; the seeker of wealth and power, who passed here for a pursuer of substance, would grasp a shadow.

17\. The hints I have thrown out may, I think, enable readers to reconcile the three documents we have on the life of Socrates. Take first the Aristophanic portrait. It is a broad, extravagant caricature, but drawn by a consummate artist who, even in distorting his original's expression, shows that he studied it. I could not say this if, as some commentators pretend, he had represented Socrates as a natural philosopher. The name of the play is almost the only excuse for that notion. Who that knows the genius of Aristophanes, or the delicacy of Athenian taste, will suspect him of the wretched conceit that a man worships the clouds because he likes gazing at the stars, or his audience of tolerating it? Far more likely the airy nymphs the philosopher is said to have put in place of his country's gods are the patronesses of attempts to catch the thin, evanescent meanings, and shadows of meanings, of words. Those attempts might plausibly be imputed to one who prized philology for its own sake and found it indispensable in his warfare with the Sophists. The basket in which the philosopher hangs between heaven and earth, to mingle his thoughts with the congenial air, does not show that the poet feared Socrates saw himself as a mere particle of the world's general life, longing to be reunited with his native element. It points instead to the doctrine of the withdrawment of the spirit from the world's phantasms, which I have called so capital an article of his moral creed, and of which his idea of the soul after death is only the expansion and fulfilment. The maps and geometrical instruments the old Athenian found in the phrontisterium partly prove that Socrates often used illustrations from subjects familiar to Athenian youth, and partly seem a joke at the attempt to reduce morality to a science. The dialogue about the cause of thunder is clearly meant far more as a caricature of his method of discourse than as an exposition of his opinions. Its chief object is to leave the impression that Socrates put some special demon of his own in place of Jupiter, which the poet, to keep his metaphors consistent and to strike an oblique blow at the really physical speculators, calls Dinos. These remarks are necessary in justification of Aristophanes, for if in these parts he meant to show Socrates as a naturalist, the whole plot is absurd. Why should Strepsiades go to a natural philosopher to learn to cheat his creditors? How could such a teacher give Pheidippides lessons in beating his father? The most remarkable feature of the play, the contest between the just and the unjust principle, is decisive as to his meaning. The satirist, with the quick intuition one would expect of such an Athenian, saw that the conflict between an uplooking and a downlooking mind was the most characteristic and important feature of the system he ridiculed.

18\. The one point in the life of Socrates of which Aristophanes was utterly ignorant is its object, and this is just the point on which Xenophon is anxious to inform us. This worthy disciple is too anxious to show Socrates in his dignity, so we miss the hearty humorist who can be seen, though disguised, in the comedian's picture. It was natural that a soldier should be more struck by the positive conclusions Socrates reached on practical matters than by his method of reaching them. It was equally natural that a professed apologist should want to show his master in the way most intelligible to plain persons, who had been puzzled by reports of his strange argumentation and fancied some great mischief lurked in it. But if we bear this in mind and treat Xenophon as the expounder of the Socratic discipline rather than of the man or his principles, we shall be more struck by the agreements than by the differences between him and the other biographers. Homage to an invisible guide and teacher, the distinction between the principle in man that looks upward and the one that gravitates to the earth, and the recognition of restraints on the animal nature as means of freeing the true man, we shall find on every page of the *Memorabilia*. Standing alone, Xenophon would be unsatisfactory, even misleading; his Socrates would be almost as much a mere bundle of fine qualities or true opinions as his Cyrus. But he is most useful in giving clearness and steadiness to the apprehensions we draw from other, and on the whole better, sources. In him we see clearly that Socrates kept a moral end before him from first to last, and was to all intents a practical man. This discovery, instead of making the other accounts harder to interpret, which some think inconsistent with it, makes them more intelligible and more consistent with themselves than we would otherwise have thought.

19\. In the Socrates of Plato we find the Aristophanic and the Xenophontic Socrates, the mere humorist and debater and the mere moralist, uniting to form the real man. It has often been said that Plato's brilliant imagination created a hero with very few points of resemblance to the actual Socrates. It would certainly be hopeless to clear Plato of the charge of a brilliant, and more than brilliant, imagination. But the word has two meanings. If it means contempt of reason and probability, the gift must belong in a much lower degree to Plato than to those who think it possible that a man living in the very city where Socrates had walked and talked for years could palm off on his countrymen a false or fantastic image of him. If instead we mean the power of giving substance and life to what would otherwise be a mere shadow, it must be a most serviceable ally to one who would collect and harmonise the remembrances of an actual character, no less than to one who would create a character that never existed. Strong affection may make such a faculty unnecessary in a mere biographer, or perhaps awaken it. But one who has to describe a friend's thoughts, words, and acts, and also to show how they bore on the state of his country and will bear on men's speculations and lives for ages, needs no ordinary measure of this faculty. This is Plato's work. He proposed to exhibit Socrates as the guide into a particular line and course of thought. To do it, he had to bring him livingly before us, so that we should see not his opinions but himself, trace the workings of his mind, and see how he acted on others and they on him. By no other means could Plato have given us the true Socrates, and without presenting the true Socrates he could never have brought out clearly the different sides of his own philosophy.

#### SECTION III. THE SOCRATICS.

1\. Three schools grew directly out of the philosophy and discipline of Socrates, usually called the Cyrenaic, the Cynic, and the Megaric. They may be called the parents of the most conspicuous theories that occupied later Greece. The Cyrenaic doctrine, mixed with a tributary stream from the physics of Democritus, ended in Epicurism. The Cynic joined with the Megarian to form Stoicism. The Megarian also contributed one element to the speculations that flourished much later at Alexandria. It is worth tracing the leading thoughts of each and showing how they began with Socrates.

2\. Aristippus of Cyrene seems to have had a singularly easy, happy temperament. Pleasures did not excite him, and pains passed lightly over him. One would have thought few men could have had less sympathy with Socrates, who fought hard all his life with himself and the world. Yet this earnest thinker had charms even for Aristippus. Socrates said we are not to yield to circumstances but to master them, and Aristippus, whom no circumstance oppressed, found in his own experience an interpretation of that maxim. Socrates often pitied and warned the thoughtless man for his restlessness and perturbation. What more natural inference than that freedom from annoyance, a dismissal of all anxious and turbulent cares, is the great end of philosophy? Besides these well-known commonplaces of his master's talk, Aristippus could no doubt cite authentic fragments of conversation in which Socrates seemed to assume pleasure as the end of life and to fit his other maxims to it. He could tell, we may be sure, of times when Socrates rebuked his own lazy, voluptuous habits of mind and yet seemed to aim at proving, not that they led to a wrong end, but that they were ill-chosen means to it. So Aristippus easily persuaded himself that he was a Socratic, indeed the best and most complete interpreter of Socrates, when he announced the great discovery that pleasure and pain are the ultimate principles of human life, and that pursuing the one and avoiding the other is, and must be, every man's business. Whatever honour belongs to the first formal proclaimer of a doctrine that has held so prominent a place in the philosophy of every age must be given to Aristippus. What distinguishes him from later and less practical reasoners of this class is his plain and honest assertion that the momentary, concrete gratification, not the complex notion of happiness, is and must be the object of men's desires and labours. It was easy for him to fit other parts of the Socratic creed to this. If choosing the agreeable and rejecting the disagreeable is the great virtue of the soul, how conveniently could Socrates' language on the connection of virtue with reason and knowledge serve the new sect! It is the intellect that prefers and discards, and why should these acts of judgment not be the same as that perception of what is and what is not to which the master so constantly referred? As for the apparent self-restraint and bodily privations of Socrates, they were no real contradiction to the Cyrenaic theory, which allows all varieties of taste and may let one man seek mental pleasure at the cost of bodily, and another bodily at the cost of mental. The school went through several changes. With Theodorus, pleasure and pain ceased to be real outward objects, and self-seeking and self-glorification became the acknowledged ends of the wise man. In Hegesias the hope of attaining pleasure gave way to a mere devising of ways to avoid pain. Anniceris seems to have smoothed the rough edges of the doctrine and prepared the way for its merging in the more general notions of happiness matured by Epicurus.

3\. The Cynic school, as it appears in its founder Antisthenes and in Diogenes, its only very notorious disciple, is the formal opposite of the Cyrenaic. Yet it adds one more illustration to the old maxim that Mr. Coleridge observed to be the most pregnant of all for the philosopher and the historian of philosophy: "Extremes meet." Both started from the same Socratic maxim, and probably both cited the same discourses in defence of their systems. The wise man should not submit to circumstances but rule them, said Aristippus, and so his whole business is to arrange them for the most pleasure and least pain. A man should be superior to his circumstances, said Antisthenes, and therefore must overcome his sensitivity to pleasure and pain and try to live wholly within himself, cultivating the nobler part of him that outward impulses and impressions do not touch. If Aristippus could cite passages from the discourses of Socrates, Antisthenes could appeal more confidently to the whole course of his life, his habitual struggle for victory over mere sensations. The Cynics were really disciplinarians more than doctrinists. They had a hard dogmatism of their own, but they were far more eager to show their own indifference to passing accidents than to find principles and reasons for it. Of the two, Antisthenes seems to have been the more honest and Diogenes the more original. The first was hard and narrow but apparently sincere; the second was an ostentatious coxcomb, whose proud, insolent spirit now and then threw off sparks of what might have been genius had it come with simplicity of character and a true purpose.

4\. Euclides of Megara was unquestionably more sagacious and subtle than any of those named. He was drawn to Socrates not by hope of a theory of life or a scheme of self-culture, but by Socrates' unrivalled skill in disputation. Had he lived thirty years earlier, he would have been an Eleatic or a Sophist. But nothing shows the effect of Socratic teaching on the Greek mind more remarkably than the moral tone it gave to men who would otherwise have been mere debaters. To argue was the taste and vocation of the Megarian school, but their arguments were all drawn irresistibly to the question, "What is the Good?" Pursuing it, they were led naturally to the pregnant positions of Socrates about evil as a departure from, and rebellion against, what *is*, which, as we have seen, was the ultimate and in one way the most characteristic part of his philosophy. This principle, cut off from the living processes by which Socrates reached it and sought to make others conscious of it, and set in naked opposition to every other idea of virtue or goodness, made up the Megarian doctrine. All their labour went to disproving the obvious and apparently irresistible opinion that what the senses report is the most real and certain. We have heard how Zeno defended the doctrine of his friend and master Parmenides by showing the utter instability of sensible presumptions and conclusions. The Megarian school used the same method. The difference lay in the character of the periods: Zeno's purpose was to support the metaphysical idea of Oneness, the Megarians' to support the moral idea of absolute, unchangeable Being.

5\. The history of this school is melancholy and instructive. Euclides, though his mind leaned to disputation, felt the grandeur of the moral lessons he had learned from Socrates. In Eubulides positive faith was displaced by delight in his own subtlety and in refuting opposing arguments. Diodorus Cronos admired and revered the mere forms of the understanding, apart from all vital principles or results. Finally Stilpo seems to have lost the characteristic idea of the Megarian school altogether while carrying its characteristic weakness to the highest point. His problem was not to establish the existence of objective truth but to show how an intellect might be formed that would be most impassive to influences from without and least disturbed by feelings from within. One of his pupils was Zeno of Cittium, the author of Stoicism.

#### SECTION IV. PLATO.

1\. The biographer of the Greek philosophers reports that Socrates once dreamed he had an unfledged cygnet on his knee. In a few moments it grew wings and flew away, singing very sweetly. The next day a young man came to him who was said to count Solon among his nearer ancestors, and through Solon to look back to Codrus and the god Poseidon. His name was Plato.

2\. Before coming to Socrates, this youth had written dithyrambs, songs and tragedies. He had studied under Ariston of Argos, a celebrated wrestler. Some say he got his name from the breadth of his chest, and that he won a wrestling prize at the Isthmian games. Whatever his earlier studies, the day he settled at the knees of Socrates decided the course of his life. Nothing he had learnt before was wasted, but the discourses of Socrates gave it meaning and direction. From Socrates he learnt to understand himself, and so to understand his predecessors and contemporaries. He learnt what a Greek, and what a man, ought to seek, and what perils and temptations beset either who starts the search.

3\. Plato has so identified himself with his master that it is hard to establish the events of his own life. Less is recorded of him than of many insignificant Greek sophists, and what is recorded rests on poor evidence. The epistles that bear his name have long been rejected as spurious, though some scraps of information may be drawn from them. The most interesting concern his journeys to Sicily: his connection with the elder tyrant Dionysius, his attempts to reform the younger, his hope of realising an ideal polity through a dissolute and worthless tyrant, and his influence on Dion, the stern aristocrat, conspirator and despot. We cannot arrange this history, but we may grant that Plato had an intense longing to prove he was no mere dreamer, and that what he believed could be realised. We have too few facts to draw a moral about a noble mind yielding to hopes of great results from such instruments. He may never have had flattering expectations, but only, perhaps reluctantly, carried out a task laid on him. How far it was *necessary* that his polity be tried in Syracuse or anywhere to test its principles, we can judge better once we have considered what it was. The question that concerns us now is whether Plato, once fledged, flew off into the air and left his master on the earth, as some have fancied and the old tradition seems to hint; or whether he was the truest expounder of his master — the true Socratic precisely because he was not the founder of a Socratic system but a living and original investigator.

4\. Plato conversed with both Aristippus and Antisthenes. With Euclides he was closer, for after the death of Socrates, when Athens seemed unsafe, he and other disciples fled to Megara. It would be rash to say that his own thought was shaped by watching these three, since internal evidence apparently supports the view that his *Phædrus* and *Laches* were written in his master's lifetime. Yet he must have noticed very early how his fellow-disciples seized on particular sayings of their teacher and built systems and schools out of them, and he must have thought deeply about how to escape the same temptation. If Socrates had made up his creed from the systems then current in Greece, it would be no surprise that the elements, artificially put together, reasserted their independence and were claimed by the minds they suited. Everything Plato had seen of his master made that supposition impossible. Whether or not Socrates had studied other schools, every thought he uttered came fresh and living from himself — or rather was the joint fruit of his own reflection and that of the people he talked with. He could minister to other minds because he knew so well what passed in his own, and sought out each principle as the answer to a real difficulty. But every philosopher is in some sort an inquirer into the workings of his own mind, and his philosophy, if sincere, shows that mind. How then was Socrates, so remarkably *himself*, kept from the narrowness and exclusiveness into which Aristippus and Antisthenes, both sincere in their way, had plainly fallen?

5\. Plato could answer only that it was the healthy habit of always tying his thoughts to outward circumstances and to the puzzles of his own age that kept the Socratic doctrines, in their owner's hands, from stagnating into mere theory. The obvious way to make a philosophy complete and fit for all times is to strip off the accidental features that suited it to one crisis. Plato was convinced by reflection and experience that the opposite course was the safe one. Homer could be read and enjoyed in the age of Pericles not because he stood aloof from the local and temporary, but because he was wrapped in it; men were presented as they were in a quite different state of manners, and that was exactly what let readers see them as brothers and countrymen. The reader will think of many reasons why the analogy of poetry does not hold for philosophy; it is enough that they did not weigh with Plato. No one knew or felt more strongly the essential difference between poetry and philosophy — he was even betrayed into exaggerations in asserting it. But he was convinced it did not lie in this, that the poet wins immortality by adapting himself to the feelings of his age and the philosopher by divesting himself of them all. He thought he saw that giving up living and practical sympathies, divorcing oneself from human interests, produces the very narrowness the philosopher hopes to escape. So if Greek wisdom was not to fall back from where Socrates had brought it, or ever to serve other countries and periods, Plato concluded it must not dissolve into speculations or declamations about this scheme of life or that principle of action. It must show itself in the conversations of actual men — not of some imaginary day, but of that day — talking about what they really talked of when they met in the streets or at their feasts. He took no pains to forget the people he lived among or what occupied them, or to adopt any more universal way of thinking and speaking than theirs.

6\. The *Dialogue* of Plato is therefore not, as some have said, an artistic invention in which the philosopher sacrificed severe judgment to imagination, to a reputation for dramatic skill, or to the wish to present truths agreeably. He plainly regarded it as a necessary means of making clear the truths he believed he possessed, and he is anxious above all to impress this, that in the Dialogue rightly used we have the induction to all principles. It is strange that he should be accused of sacrificing his disciples to a selfish desire for fame by a method that leads them step by step in self-inquiry, or of using it to win their favour, when it caused more vexation to every superficial talker of the day than anything else his genius could have devised. A mere artist tries to carry us at once into noble contemplations that make us conscious of our own greatness.
