He would have told them that if ever the study in which the heathen sage forgot his usual wisdom should become the only one in which Christians care to excel, and if ever, "from the opening of the ways of the senses and the kindling of a greater light of nature, some unbelief and night should arise in our minds toward the divine mysteries," then a feeling would grow up toward those mysteries worse than unbelief, if it is not another form of it: a stupid acquiescence in them, without acknowledging that they answer any craving of the heart, any necessity of reason, any prediction of the imagination. In that case, for the sake of the age and country so diseased, of all who should follow, and of physical knowledge itself, which can never long flourish apart from moral light, it would be most desirable that men resume the study of the Athenian philosopher. They should realise the wants of their own minds by observing those he felt in his, and consider how an adequate provision for both can be found.

### DIVISION III.—ARISTOTLE.

#### SECTION I. ARISTOTLE THE BEGINNER OF A NEW EPOCH.

1\. When we call Plato the ideal philosopher, we forget that the Greek people, his fellow-citizens especially, were pursuing ideals the whole time he and his master lived. Socrates was born in 468 B.C. and Plato died in 347 B.C. Pericles entered public life a year before Socrates was born, and Philip of Macedon took Olynthus the year Plato died. If one expression must stand for these 120 years, it is this: sculptors, painters, poets, politicians, cities and mobs were all occupied with some ideal of beauty, wisdom, freedom or self-government. They were striving to realise it, dreaming of it, or playing with it to bewilder others. The philosopher belonged to mankind, but he belonged just as remarkably to his own time. He interpreted, ordered and justified its cravings, and showed that they had a true foundation and must have an ultimate satisfaction.

2\. A different stage of history clearly begins when Philip appears. His name signals that the age of individual energy had passed. In that age, weighty events took place in insignificant localities, and the pettiest party contests developed the most lasting and universal principles. His name marks the arrival of an age of concentrated organising power, of successful assaults on freedom, of grand conceptions and wide conquests, of what has well been called material sublimity. This age needed its own philosophical expounder and representative. One was provided, who was destined to influence later times more than his own.

3\. In 367 a young man arrived in Athens who had been born at Stagira in Chalcidice. His father was a physician at the court of Amyntas II., king of Macedonia. The youth had been brought to that court and had met Philip, the king's son, who was a few years younger. But at seventeen no court attraction could keep him from the city where Plato lived and where all wisdom was to be found. Perhaps he was almost ashamed of a country that Athenians still affected to think semi-barbarous.

4\. No scrupulous Athenian could have found anything barbarous in the young Aristotle. A defect in his pronunciation was only a lisp. He differed from the ordinary student in taking more care of his person: he set off his short, slender figure with somewhat fastidious dress, and, it is reported, with rings and a fashionable haircut. Small quick eyes and a sarcastic curl of the lips were noted as his characteristics, perhaps the only important ones, until Plato returned from his Sicilian journey, during which he seems to have been absent. Plato found the most promising pupil his school had ever had, and surnamed him "the reader." He found it necessary to check this pupil's philosophical devotion rather than excite it.

5\. Even so, Speusippus, not Aristotle, succeeded Plato in the Academy. Aristotle left Athens just when Philip's triumphs were becoming terrible to the liberties of Greece. Five years later he was at Philip's court, educating his son Alexander. His influence on Alexander's mind must have been prodigious, and Alexander's whole later history depended on the years between his fourteenth and seventeenth. He came to his master a raw, untamed youth, with impulses no one could understand or govern. He left as a Greek prince who united the vigour and accomplishments of the republican period with his father's schemes and capacities, a desire to conquer the world, and an intellect able to achieve it. We need not ask whether Aristotle inspired or favoured Alexander's particular projects. Even if he discouraged them, the direction of mind that made them conceivable and achievable came from the teacher. Without this culture, Alexander might have aspired to no more than victory in one of those Olympic contests from which a not very remote ancestor had been excluded.

6\. Alexander gave his master the one reward he really prized. He enabled him to make his history of animals a book of instruction and wonder for all later generations, Cuvier and Owen among them. Every nation Alexander conquered enriched Aristotle with new and more valuable facts. Aristotle stored up and meditated on these treasures in Athens, where all his great works were written. There he gathered a circle of devoted pupils who heard both his more popular and his more systematic teaching. There he commented on his predecessors, corresponded with Alexander, and endured the misunderstandings of Alexander and his court. He suffered domestic sorrows, which he felt as a man and which give us a personal interest in him. Finally he underwent the popular charge of impiety, which had banished Anaxagoras and killed Socrates. Summoned before the Areopagus on this charge, he declined to appear and was condemned to death. He retired to Chalcis and died there in 322 B.C.

#### SECTION II. RELATION OF ARISTOTLE TO PLATO.

1\. A student who passes from Plato to Aristotle is struck first by the complete absence of the dramatic form and feeling he has grown used to. The living people he conversed with are gone. Protagoras, Prodicus, and Hippias no longer lounge on their couches among admiring pupils. There are no walks along the city wall, no readings beside the Ilissus, no lively symposia leading to high talk about love, no Critias recalling the tales of ancient and glorious republics that he heard in his youth before he became a tyrant. Above all there is no Socrates at the centre of these groups, standing out in his own character and showing that the subtlest of dialecticians may be the most humorous and humane of men. Everyone will feel some sorrow at losing so many beautiful pictures. But most readers will think the precision and philosophical dignity of the treatise ample compensation for the richness of the dialogue. They like to hear solemn questions treated solemnly and opinions discussed calmly, without personalities. Above all they like a profound and considerate judge who is willing to pronounce a positive decision on the evidence. They count this a great advantage, and they find it, and more, in Aristotle.

2\. Still, I think the person who can do justice to the master's method will be the best judge of the disciple. We will understand neither well if we settle for a vague notion that one was a consummate artist and the other a profound practical philosopher. I have already argued that Plato used the dialogue not for artistic purposes but because his idea of science required it. In the same way, to claim for Aristotle his true and noble position, we must not suppose that his claim to be practical or profound rests on lacking Plato's qualities or abandoning Plato's method. We call those studies most practical that are tied to real, living, passing questions. Yet it was the actual opposition of the Sophists that drove Socrates and Plato to seek principles not yet recognised, lest they lose those they had. Aristotle could examine sophistical arguments calmly, because it was the hour in the school for that subject. In Plato's day it was a matter of life and death whether we have anything permanent to rest on, for men in every Greek town were abusing Heraclitus's name to support a doctrine of perpetual flux. Aristotle could label the question physical or metaphysical and balance it patiently against an opposing theory. The Parmenideans forced Plato to investigate the nature and conditions of science, because they threatened it with hopeless stillness. Aristotle feels no such alarm; he has only to compose a system of analytics. Socrates, whose body was about to pass through death, was led to consider the nature and lasting properties of the soul. Aristotle opens his treatise on the soul by asking whether it is to be considered in relation to any particular person or in the abstract, and whether we should speak of it physically or dialectically. Without deciding which course is best, I think it would violate ordinary usage to call the latter the more practical.

3\. Nor is depth quite the quality to attribute to Aristotle when we contrast him with his predecessor. Plato's situation and task made him always seek principles. The simplest everyday facts puzzled him, and nothing that interested human beings was beneath his notice, but what he kept asking about was the meaning of these things, the truth implied in them. The commonest word and the commonest act were unintelligible to him except by a light from another region than the one we habitually live in. There is no trace of this feeling in Aristotle. His ambition, and perhaps his appointed function, was to collect every possible fact and arrange and classify it. No one is less tempted to suspect a deep meaning in facts, or to grope after one. Likewise his highest aim with words is to press and settle them into a definition. The thought that words have a life, connected with the life in us, and might help interpret its marvels, never entered his mind, or at least never stayed there. This disposition may bring comfort and advantage, but people careful with language would not call it "profound."

4\. Another prejudice must be removed if we are to understand their relative positions. It is often fancied, and Aristotle seems not anxious to correct the impression, that Plato's disciple forsook him as Plato had forsaken his master, and that the later philosophy is in some respects a return to the simple faith of Socrates. If what I have said is right, this notion is not merely wide of the truth but the opposite of it. Plato's personality was exactly the quality of mind and writing that he inherited from Socrates. He seldom drifted into abstractions, and he kept the strong feeling that "we are actual men, wrestling against evil tendencies within and evil powers without, capable of being educated, and of educating each other into a longing after, and perception of, the perfect Goodness and Truth." He owed this to Socrates. His own work was to connect this personal struggle with the orderly development of principles. It was just with the Socrates in Plato that Aristotle could not sympathise. I can easily imagine that he had a general respect for Socrates's good sense, that he recognised him as the useful and victorious opponent of what was mischievous and unphilosophical, and that he sincerely believed Socrates did not hold certain offensive opinions of his disciple. But I cannot believe that he in the least admired the Socratic method, or that all his wisdom could teach him where that method must lead anyone who habitually followed it.

5\. These remarks may seem for a moment to disparage this wonderful man, but on reflection they relieve his character of some unjust charges, set his real merits in a clearer light, and explain the kind of influence he has had and always will have. Some over-watchful, sensitive critics find in his works passages showing personal jealousy and dislike of Plato. They say he does not comment on Plato in a manly, philosophical spirit, but usually with affected phrases of reluctance, which betray hidden ill-will more than strong abuse would. Perhaps these passages would have seemed far less meaningful if the gossiping anecdote-mongers of later Greece had not illustrated them with stories of quarrels between master and pupil. These stories plainly come from vulgar invention or a trivial, treacherous memory, yet once heard they influence us unawares and keep us from looking fairly at the evidence that gives them their only plausibility. Apart from the stories, the quotations prove only that Aristotle felt irritation and displeasure on perceiving something in Plato's words that his large intellect and immense information could not grasp. Imagine being haunted by this thought: "In all definable qualities I am equal, nay superior to my predecessor; I have reduced subjects into far greater order, I analyse far more perfectly, I have a far greater store of facts at my command; and yet there is in him something quite *undefinable*, which seems to make an incredible difference between us." That could well vex even an honest and great mind. It would be not only a personal humiliation but a jar against his strongest conviction, that nothing ought to be incapable of definition and that whatever defies it can scarcely be of great worth. So admirers and flatterers, equally unable to understand either man, may have stirred up petty quarrels between them. But we do not need that supposition to account for the sneers and taunts, if such they are, that now and then displease us in Aristotle.

6\. Given all this, it is not hard to show what was peculiar to the Stagirite's philosophy: how it grew out of Plato's, how far they contradict each other, and how far one fills a space the other left empty. We have seen how Plato came to his high estimate of dialectics. He watched his master hold a safe moral position against the Sophists' attacks. To assert realities against appearances and counterfeits was *his* single aim, and keeping it steadily in view he almost unconsciously worked out a method entirely different from earlier philosophers'. Reflecting on Socrates's end, he saw the full practical meaning of what Xenophanes and others had asserted in terms, that Being is the object of all our inquiries. He saw also that the end must be joined to the method, since until the method was practised, Being had been a word, a notion, a negation, not an object really to be beheld and striven after. Hence it was vital to bring the method forward and present it to his pupils substantively, not merely as leading to certain results but as the safe and universal means of reaching any results. I have mentioned a class of dialogues with this purpose. These, or something answering to them (perhaps nearer ordinary exposition, though I can scarcely believe Plato ever abandoned the dialogue as his vehicle), must have been the peculiar study of the Academy, especially of the more advanced disciples.[^fn025] So a feeling about dialectics will have grown up among these pupils that Plato would have wished to discourage, yet which his own works kept fostering. They saw it used as a key to the secrets of social, moral, and physical life, and saw their master take pains that they should examine the wards of the key. It was natural that they should think a fuller and more systematic development of this all-important science desirable, even necessary. They would see confusion and irregularity in their teacher's proceedings. Had he not strangely mixed inquiries into the grounds of morality with statements about the nature of science? Surely it would be more orderly to keep these questions distinct, each under its own head. And were there not signs of narrowness in Plato that a more accurate habit of distinction would have cured? His dislike of some usual abuses of rhetoric had led him to undervalue the whole art, though it could, like any other, be reduced to strict laws and be contemplated apart from its accidental results. The same might be said of his doctrine of poetry, and still more of his ill-concealed indifference to physical speculation. If all these subjects were looked at directly as distinct branches of human culture, how much knowledge might be gained in each, and how much clearer we might become about the capacities and limits of the human intellect!

[^fn025]: Mr. Blakesley's Life of Aristotle (in the Encyclopædia Metropolitana) shows, I think most satisfactorily, that Aristotle's *acroamatic* treatises differed from the *exoteric* not in the abstruseness or mysteriousness of their subject matter, but in that the former formed part of a course or system, while the latter were casual discussions or lectures on a particular thesis. The remark in the text extends and adapts this doctrine to Plato.

7\. Such thoughts may have been at work in many who frequented Plato's school. In few did they bear fruit, and in most of those few it was the unripe or blighted fruit of feeble theories that professed to universalise Plato's system and really showed their authors knew nothing of Plato or of themselves. But one was able to make the others' thoughts intelligible. To him Platonism will have seemed a necessary preparation for a complete, circular philosophy. Its unsystematic character, imaginative flights, and neglect of certain provinces of thought will have looked like signs of rudeness and infancy. He will have conceived the thought of assigning each study its true place, with the one Plato had proved so important in the first place, shown in full proportion and determining the character and treatment of the rest. So dialectics was in a sense the centre of both philosophies. Nor did Aristotle consciously change what the word meant in his predecessor's discourses. He only wanted to give the study more distinctness and prominence, and to exhibit its processes and operations apart from any particular applications and results. But in doing this the character of the pursuit inevitably changed. Plato's feeling was: certain objects are presented to my mind, whether sensible objects like trees or objects for the understanding like names, but objects still, things *thrown in my way*; I must know what they mean and find out their truth, and for this I must have dialectics. For Aristotle the object has vanished from before his eyes and mind, and he devotes his whole energy to contemplating dialectics in itself. What follows? The sense of needing dialectics as an escape from the impositions that block our view of things as they are grows weaker and at last disappears. Plato spent his life asserting against Protagoras and his school that the mind is not its own standard, that the aspects under which objects appear to us are not our knowledge of them, and that we can know them as they are in themselves. This principle gave his dialectics all its meaning, and his disciple no longer feels it with any force. On the contrary, Aristotle proposes to investigate precisely the aspects under which we see and judge things. He wants to know the rules and conditions under which the mind, by its own constitution, considers and discourses. He makes the mind a centre, referring everything to itself, as Plato's opponents did. But he differed from them in that their intention was knavish and his entirely honest. They set up the doctrine that things are only as they seem to us in order to unsettle all faith and make each individual's judgment a lawful standard. He sought to convince men that all is not unstable and fluctuating, by showing a fixed rule to which human judgments must conform. That rule limits individual taste and caprice, and tests and orders the appearances that the Sophist pretended were infinite.

8\. So the definition of dialectics in Plato and Aristotle may be almost the same, while the whole scope and object of the science is different. Either could say that dialectics is the science that discovers the difference between false and true. But in Plato the false is the semblance an object presents to the sensualised mind, and the true is the object's very substance and meaning. In Aristotle the false is a wrong *affirmation* about whatever the mind takes cognizance of, and the true is a right *affirmation* about the same. So Plato's dialectic treats of how we gain a clear and vital perception of things, and Aristotle's of how we discourse about them. To Plato, words are the means by which we ascend to realities that have no sensible exponents. To Aristotle they are formulas in which we set out our notions and judgments. One wants to find what hidden meaning a word indexes; the other wants to keep the word from crossing boundaries he has fixed for it. Hence the sense and leading maxim of Plato's philosophy became not only more distasteful but positively more unintelligible to his wisest disciple than to many who had never studied in the Academy or had set themselves against it. When Aristotle had matured his dialectics, it seemed so perfect and satisfactory that he could not dream of anything outside its circle that could not be brought under its rules. He felt he had found all the forms in which any proposition can be set down in words. What more there could be, what opening for another region wholly outside the government of these forms, he had no conception. If there were such a region it must be a vague, uninhabited world, and to suppose it peopled with other, most real and distinct forms was the extravagance of philosophical delirium. So when he speaks of the doctrine of substantial ideas, ideas that are the grounds of all our forms of thought and so cannot be subject to them, he is driven to a strange and, for so consummate a logician, most disagreeable necessity: he begs the whole question. He argues that since these ideas ought to come under some ascertained condition of logic, and by hypothesis come under none, they must be fictitious.

9\. As we go on I shall note how this primary difference affected the two philosophers' views on every question they took up. For now I mention it to show that methods with entirely different objects need not interfere with each other. The Platonic doctrine is not absurd because Aristotle could find no place for it in his system. And Aristotle's labours are not useless or ill-directed because they do not supply, as he fancied they did, any satisfaction for the inquiries Plato had awakened.

#### SECTION III. THE LOGICAL TREATISES OF ARISTOTLE.

1\. Any orderly examination of Aristotle must, I think, begin with his treatises on logic. They are not the most interesting of his works, but without some grasp of their nature and purpose we can hardly understand the character, value and necessary limits of his opinions on ethics, politics, rhetoric and poetry. I shall shortly quote an eminent writer on physical science to show that a fair estimate of Aristotle's work in that field depends on knowing how much weight he gave to the forms of logic. The long-debated question closer to this sketch is what exactly he meant by Metaphysics, or which of his thoughts his disciples referred to by that name. I think it can be settled only by first examining his dialectics.

2\. In the Berlin edition of Aristotle the *Categories* come first. Doubts about its genuineness seem to have been removed by modern inquiry. A work that exactly fills a gap in an author's acknowledged writings, so that together they form a complete system, would often be suspect. Against that, roundness is Aristotle's great characteristic. It is easier to imagine a perfect series of his logical writings reaching us than a pupil able to fill a gap he left. It is also less hard to credit one man with such mastery of the subject when we remember that the original conception of the study was Zeno's, not his. I have already explained how naturally that conception arose in a mind that had taken in the principle of Xenophanes and Parmenides: that the mind has laws of its own and is independent of what the senses report. What more we owe to Zeno beyond this discovery and its use in refuting sensible conclusions, we do not know. Some Sophists, though they turned the art to the worst ends, may have refined and improved it. In the school of Euclides the principles of logic, not only its practice, must have been studied and clarified. Given these materials, it is not incredible that a man trained in Plato's school to great subtlety and precision, and possessing unparalleled breadth and diligence, produced a design and edifice that later ages have scarcely been able to alter or improve. He did not raise the science from its foundations. "He found it brick and left it marble" may be said of him as truly as of almost any architect.

3\. The work on the *Categories* seems a fitting vestibule to this building. On entering it we feel that its purpose is altogether different from what Plato taught us to think all-important, and yet that it is a true purpose. There is a way of penetrating the nature and essence of things, whether they present an outward image to my senses or, just as really, speak only to my mind. Aristotle does not concern himself with that way. But our minds certainly form notions about both kinds of things, and these notions may themselves be subject to rules. They may be defined and classified, and there may be a general set of conceptions to which all particular ones refer. Aristotle affirms this. He says all our notions can be reduced to ten: Substance, Quantity, Quality, Relation, Time, Place, Position, Possession, Action, Passion. A Platonist will ask what these words themselves signify. How do I know what Substance is better than I know what a man or a horse is? Or Quantity better than I know what three cubits long is? These questions had to be asked, as we shall see, and were asked to good effect when the Aristotelian province of thought tried to bring all other provinces within it. But for any purpose other than destroying that pretension they are beside the point. When I study an actual man or horse, the substance is doubtless the unknown *x* I am looking for, and to assume I know it is to stop investigation. But I understand the *name* substance as well as the *name* man or horse. And who says that, because there is a science of *things*, there is no science of *names*, no laws of dependence and affinity among them? Conformity or nonconformity to such laws is exactly what we mean by coherent or incoherent discourse. We must either call our deep desire for arrangement and classification a mere disease, or believe it arises from the limits and conditions to which our minds are subject, and is another name for the wish to understand them.

4\. The rules of grammar, the terminology of every art and science, the very attempt to be intelligible, all presuppose these limits. Men keep inventing divisions and schemes of thought, and keep narrowing and stifling great principles and laws by them. There is no safety from this except a clear and steady perception of certain necessary boundaries, imposed not by our fellow-men but by the nature of our own understandings. Let the reader consider the *Categories* carefully. Does it not clear his mind, and in no ordinary degree, about his own modes of speech? Does it not make him feel more than before that his words, winged as they are, cannot take a chance flight but must follow an appointed, preordained path? Is there not, then, a witness in himself that Aristotle has a distinct and reasonable end which it is greatly in our interest to know?

5\. From these general forms under which all our notions fall, Aristotle goes on, in his treatise *On Interpretation*, to inquire into the mode of our affirmations and denials. There he develops the nature and limits of propositions, the meaning of contraries and contradictories, and the force of affirmation and denial in impossible, contingent and necessary matter. I have no excuse for dwelling on works of merely formal character. I mention them to press on the reader Archbishop Whately's important remark that the two books of the *Prior Analytics* develop the syllogistic principle and process. Aristotle is not the mere inventor of an art but the masterly expounder of the facts on which the art rests, and without which it would have no meaning. He does not teach us how to make propositions, but what propositions are and necessarily must be, given the conditions of the human intellect. He does not tell us how to make syllogisms, but how we do syllogise when we do not violate the laws of our mind, as we would violate the laws of our body by trying to walk on our heads instead of our feet.

6\. But Aristotle saw that this analysis of our mental operations was not enough. He had told us how we *discourse*, not how we *know*. Are these forms of logic themselves knowledge? Is syllogistic demonstration the same as science? Or is only one kind of it science? Or are its results science? Or are there premises assumed in it which also do, or may, belong to science? What are they, and how do we get at them? These are the questions that occupy him in the *Posterior Analytics*. I shall try to seize a few points in the inquiry that will best let the reader judge Aristotle's mind and see how he stands related both to his great master and to the expounder of the inductive philosophy.

7\. The treatise opens: "All teaching and all intellectual learning come from previously existing knowledge. This is plain from looking at every case. The mathematical sciences are acquired this way, and so is each of the other arts. So it is also with arguments, both by syllogism and by induction: both teach through what is already known, the one taking its premises as from those who understand, the other showing the universal through the clearness of the particular. Rhetoricians persuade in the same way, either through examples, which is induction, or through enthymemes, which is syllogism." Two important words in the first clause, *teaching* and *learning*, were, I imagine, carefully distinguished in the school Aristotle came from. I am confident that *learning*, being the method of acquisition, would there have taken precedence of *teaching*, which points to the communication of knowledge. That here the order is changed, and teaching put first as if it contained learning, is very significant and explains much that follows. Plato had a profound reverence for mathematics: "Let no man undisciplined in geometry enter the halls of philosophy." I cannot account for this unless he saw in the mathematical process something akin to his own method. That resemblance does not lie in what we call mathematical demonstration, the machinery of axioms, definitions, hypotheses and propositions, which, valuable as it is, has scarcely a Socratic element in it. What remains? Plato, I think, would answer: exactly the essence of mathematics, the *learning*. Demonstration is only the *teaching*, a necessary and inseparable accident of the science, which implies something else and means nothing without it. Learning is the process by which, in a particular triangle, I arrive at one of the laws belonging to it as a triangle, that its three angles equal two right angles. Teaching is the formal verbal statement of that law and its confirmation by deductions from admitted premises. In this sense mathematics would be to Plato not *the* science but the best preparation for it, because it recognises permanent forms and principles in visible objects that we are able to enter into and discover. Aristotle, as I have often hinted, perceives only the forms and laws of our mind. So what was the subordinate part of mathematics for his master became the whole for him. He looks at mathematics only in reference to demonstrations; its mark is that it deals with necessary matter. Ask why necessary, and the only answer is that it starts ultimately from self-evident propositions. So the proposition, a mode of our own mind, becomes the ultimate ground of all things; or if you want a Hercules' pillar beyond it, you have the *Categories*. Penned within these limits, it is hard to grasp what science means. Aristotle feels the difficulty and, with his usual honesty, does not evade it. He admits it may puzzle us to say whether science is only what we get after a series of satisfactory syllogisms, or whether the premises assumed in them must have the same character. All he can say is that we must deal fairly with facts, and that what we are bound to assume as known we do in some sort actually know. Ultimate knowledge, then, the most perfect truth the philosopher can reach, as well as his starting point, is still a proposition. All knowledge seems to fall under two forms: knowing *that* it is so, and knowing *why* it is so. Neither can include the knowledge Plato aims at, knowledge correlative with Being, which is not *about* things or persons but *of* them.

8\. If these forms of our mind are the ground of our knowledge, we cannot reach them at once. What are the steps? Here comes in Aristotle's doctrine about sensible objects. Our perception of them is not strictly knowledge, for that word, whether used of premises or conclusions, always refers to the universal. Our sense apprehensions refer only to particulars, to individuals. From these, which are most evident to us, we reach the more general by induction. How entirely this differs from what Bacon called induction, the reader will see the more he studies Aristotle. He will find first that the sensible *phenomenon* is taken for granted as a safe starting point. Aristotle believed as strongly as we do that phenomena are not principles. But to suspect phenomena, to suppose they need sifting and probing to learn what fact they denote, is no part of his system. The sensible impression satisfied him, not to rest in, but as a true beginning; the whole difference between those who acquiesce in it and the most consummate philosopher lay in the use the latter made of his power of generalising and syllogising. In this way Aristotle became the parent of all the modern schools of sensible philosophy. Yet those schools have drawn their best and most convincing arguments from the errors into which Aristotle and the Aristotelians were led by their own hypothesis. The first book of Locke is an elaborate and satisfactory exposure of the notions into which the Stagirite school is driven by its determination to recognise no foundation of truth but sense and experience. They were too learned and thoughtful not to see that the mind demands universal forms somehow. Because they would not acknowledge them as the grounds of our mind, they were forced to seek them in the mind, and so to conceive them as propositions. Hence the fancy of an innate notion that whatever is, is, or that the whole is greater than its part. Mr. Locke confuted that fancy amid shouts of triumph from his admirers, while in fact he was conspiring with Aristotle to disparage the principle that delivers us from such fallacies. But I am anticipating a later page.

9\. These later analytics deal entirely with demonstrative reasoning. The *Topics*, a much longer work, deal wholly with probable reasoning. Here Aristotle seems to me far more at home. I have suggested that he never possessed, and could not possess, the idea of Science; it lay outside his province, and when he tried to grapple with it he brought it within forms that robbed it of its essence. But no one did so much to give scientific form to subjects that are by nature not scientific, or to give our thoughts precision about the wavering matters of opinion and ordinary conduct. Looking at the *Topics*, we confess, with some reluctance, that no method of persuasion or discourse is so loose that it cannot be analysed and shown to involve inevitable limits. We have some justification for discontent with these resolute reductions of all thought and argument to a system. We feel the vital power is not there. What really brings men to agree with a falsehood or a truth, the energy and conviction of the speaker, is left out of account. And the habit of contemplating arguments without regard to the truth they are meant to establish, or their moral influence, is somewhat hardening and deadening. But I think the work of a thorough master like Aristotle will on the whole do good. He so well understands himself and what he can do that we are not misled, as by inferior writers, into fancying nothing is wanted but what he tells us. His exquisite dissections teach what more blundering dissectors might not: that there is something which cannot be dissected. To understand Aristotle rightly, the *Topics* should be read with the three books on Rhetoric. I cannot speak of that work now, because so much of Aristotle's moral and political wisdom is presumed in it, but it is closely connected with the work on probable arguments. The *Topics* are to it what the six books of Euclid are to a treatise on practical mechanics. It may seem strange to speak of a geometry for a rhetorician, and I have shown how alien geometry was to Aristotle's habits of mind. Yet we cannot help seeing that, as far as a person with the faculty of persuasion can be taught that his is not a mere craft but has maxims and laws governing it, he will become at least less mischievous, and may become a sincere, true-minded man. From another point of view the *Topics* give the roundness and completeness to Aristotle's logical system which, as I said, he evidently desired and so remarkably achieved.

#### SECTION IV. THE PHYSICS OF ARISTOTLE.

1\. A short treatise on *Sophistical Proofs* closes the series of Aristotle's works on logic. His many writings on physics follow. We have no direct interest in these. But to show how his logical ideas shaped his views in this field, I will take the liberty of quoting a passage from Dr. Whewell's *History of the Inductive Sciences*, vol. i. sec. 2, "The Aristotelian Physical Philosophy."

2\. "The principal treatises of Aristotle are the eight books of *Physical Lectures*, the four books *Of the Heavens*, and the two books *Of Production and Destruction*. The book *Of the World* is now universally acknowledged to be spurious. The *Meteorologics* is full of physical explanations of natural phenomena, but it does not set out the doctrines and reasonings of the schools in so general a form, and the same is true of the *Mechanical Problems*. The natural-history treatises (*On Animals, On the Parts of Animals, On Plants, On Physiognomies, On Colours, On Sounds*) gather an extraordinary mass of facts and show a wonderful power of systematising. But they do not expound principles, so they need not be considered here.

3\. "The *Physical Lectures* are the works about which Simplicius, a Greek commentator of the sixth century, and Plutarch both tell a well-known anecdote. Alexander the Great is said to have written to his former tutor: 'You have not done well to publish these *Lectures*. How shall we, your pupils, excel other men if you make public what we learned from you?' Aristotle is said to have answered: 'My *Lectures* are published and not published; they will be intelligible only to those who heard them.' The story may easily have been made up and circulated by people who found the works beyond them. It cannot be denied that working out the meaning and reasoning of every part would be very laborious, if not impossible. But we can follow a large part of the work clearly enough to grasp the character and principles of its reasoning, and that is what I shall try to do.

4\. "The author's introductory account of philosophy fits closely with what has already been said: he takes his facts and generalisations as they are implied in the structure of language. 'We must in all cases proceed,' he says, 'from what is known to what is unknown.' No one will deny that, but we can hardly follow his inference. He adds, 'We must proceed, therefore, from universal to particular.' And he continues that something of this can be seen in language, for names signify things in a general and indefinite manner, as *circle* does, and by defining we unfold them into particulars. He illustrates this by saying that children at first call all men *father* and all women *mother*, and only afterwards distinguish.

5\. "Following this view, he tries to settle several great questions about the universe, raised among subtle and speculative men, by unfolding the meaning of the words and phrases applied to the most general notions of things and relations. We have noticed this method already; a few examples will illustrate it further. Rival sects had debated whether there is a void, a place without matter. The arguments were briefly these. There must be a void, because a body cannot move into a space unless it is empty, so without a void there could be no motion. On the other side, there is no void, because the intervals between bodies are filled with air, and air is something. Both opinions had even been supported by experiment. Anaxagoras and his school showed that confined air resists compression, by squeezing a blown bladder and pressing an inverted vessel down into water. On the other side, it was said that a vessel full of fine ashes holds as much water as if the ashes were not there, which could only be explained by void spaces between the ashes. Aristotle decides that there is no void, on an argument like this: in a void there could be no up and down, since in nothing there are no differences, and none in a privation or negation; but a void is merely a privation or negation of matter; therefore bodies in a void could not move up and down, which it is their nature to do. It is easy to see that such reasoning raises the familiar forms of language, and the intellectual connections of terms, above facts. It makes truth depend on whether terms are primitive, and on whether we say that bodies fall naturally. In such a philosophy every new result of observation would have to conform to the usual combinations of phrases, as they had been linked by the modes of apprehension already familiar.

6\. "I do not mean to suggest that the common modes of apprehension underlying common language are limited and casual. On the contrary, they imply universal and necessary conditions of our perceptions and conceptions: all things are necessarily apprehended as existing in time and space and as connected by cause and effect. So far as the Aristotelian philosophy reasons from these assumptions it has a real foundation, though even then its conclusions are often insecure. We have an example in the eighth book, where he says there never was a time without change and motion: 'for if all things were at rest, the first motion must have been produced by some change in some of these things; that is, there must have been a change before the first change.' Again: 'How can *before* and *after* apply where time is not? or how can time be when motion is not?' And: 'If time is a mensuration of motion, and if time be eternal, motion must be eternal.' But sometimes principles of a more arbitrary kind are brought in, and beyond the general relations of thought, the inventions of earlier speculators are taken for granted, such as the commonly received opinions about the frame of the world. From the eternity of motion, proved as above, Aristotle goes by a curious train of reasoning to identify this eternal motion with the daily motion of the heavens. 'There must,' he says, 'be something which is the first moved,' which follows from the relation of cause and effect. Then: 'Motion must go on constantly, and therefore must be either continuous or successive. Now what is continuous is more properly said to take place *constantly*, than what is successive. Also the continuous is better; but we always suppose that which is better to take place in nature, if it be possible.' Here the vague judgment of *better* and *worse* is introduced, as *natural* and *unnatural* had been before into physical reasoning.

7\. "I proceed with Aristotle's argument: 'We have now, therefore, to show that there may be an infinite, single, continuous motion, and that this is circular.' This is proved, as one might guess, from the consideration that a body may go on revolving regularly in a circle. So we have a demonstration, on the principles of this philosophy, that there is and must be a first mover, revolving eternally with uniform circular motion. This kind of philosophy may seem too trifling to dwell on, but it is important for our purpose to exemplify it so far, that we may go on knowing we have done it no injustice.

8\. "I now pass from the doctrines on the motions of the heavens to those on the material elements of the universe. Here I remark that the tendency we are tracing, to draw speculative opinions from the relations of words, must be very natural to man. The widely accepted doctrine of the four elements seems to rest on the opposition of the adjectives *hot* and *cold*, *wet* and *dry*. It is much older than Aristotle and was probably one of the earliest philosophical dogmas. But the great master of this philosophy states it more systematically than his predecessors.

9\. "'We seek,' he says, 'the principles of sensible things, that is, of tangible bodies. We must take, therefore, not all the contrarieties of quality, but those only which have reference to the touch. Thus black and white, sweet and bitter, do not differ as tangible qualities, and therefore must be rejected from our consideration.'

"'Now the contrarieties of quality which refer to the touch are these: hot, cold; dry, wet; heavy, light; hard, soft; unctuous, meagre; rough, smooth; dense, rare.' He then rejects all but the first four, for various reasons. Heavy and light go because they are not active and passive qualities; the others go because they are combinations of the first four, which he therefore infers to be the four elementary qualities.

"'Now in four things there are six combinations of two; but the combinations of two opposites, as hot and cold, must be rejected. We have, therefore, four elementary combinations, which agree with the four apparently elementary bodies: air is hot and wet (for steam is air), water is cold and wet, and earth is cold and dry.'

10\. "This disposition to assume that a common elementary quality must exist wherever we habitually apply a common adjective began before the Aristotelian philosophy and survived its reign. Among other examples, it would be hard to clear Bacon's *Inquisitio in Naturam Calidi*, 'Examination of the Nature of Heat,' of the charge of confounding very different classes of phenomena under the word *hot*. The correction of these opinions about the elementary composition of bodies belongs to an advanced period in the history of physical knowledge, even after its revival.

11\. "The Aristotelian doctrines on motion are still founded on reasoning from adjectives, but here the result follows not only from the opposition of words but from the distinction between being *absolutely* and *relatively* true. 'Former writers,' says Aristotle, 'have considered heavy and light *relatively* only, taking cases where both things have weight, but one is lighter than the other; and they imagine that in this way they defined what was *absolutely* heavy and light.' We now know that things which rise by their lightness do so only because heavier surrounding bodies press them upwards. The assumption of absolute levity is evidently gratuitous, or rather merely nominal, and it vitiated all the reasoning that followed. The inference was that fire must be absolutely light, since it tends to take its place above the other three elements, and earth absolutely heavy, since it tends to take its place below fire, air and water. The philosopher also argued with great acuteness that air, which tends to take its place below fire and above water, does so *by its nature*, and not by any combination of heavy and light elements. For if air were composed of parts that give fire its levity joined to parts that produce gravity, we could take so large a quantity of air that it would be lighter than a similar quantity of fire, having more of the light parts. It follows that each of the four elements tends to its own place: fire highest, then air, then water, and earth lowest. The whole train of errors comes from verbal fallacies: from taking light as the opposite of heavy, and levity as a quality of a body instead of an effect of surrounding bodies."

#### SECTION V. THE METAPHYSICS OF ARISTOTLE.

1\. Dr. Whewell's remarks show how Aristotle's method necessarily got in the way of true observation and interpretation of nature. They matter to us mainly because people have always felt a connection between the physical treatises and those that Aristotle, or a disciple, classed as Metaphysical. These latter writings have been much disputed. Some suppose they got their name from an accident of placement beside the books on Physics, since they could not conveniently be filed among them. Others suppose it came from Aristotle's own feeling that he was rising into a region above and beyond the one he had been living in.

2\. One of his early commentators seems to adopt both explanations. He says the object of this treatise is theological: here Aristotle "theologises." Our order of learning begins with what is last by nature, because that is better known to us. So Aristotle spoke first of physical matters, which are last by nature but first to us. The present subject is first by nature but last to us, since imperishable things are older than perishable, and the ungenerated older than the generated. Therefore he spoke first of things moved without order (in his book on *Meteors*), then of things moved according to an order or system (in the work on the *Heavens*, concerning stars and spheres), and finally, in this treatise, of things that are wholly immovable. That is theology, a study that befits the gods. So the work is entitled *After-the-Physics*: it is to be read after the physical treatises, and the title shows this. Another scholiast is more decisive: the title refers not to the character of the book but to the order of reading, since the book treats of physical *principles*. Taken together with the books themselves, these hints will, I think, explain how the two theories arose and largely reconcile them. As we proceed we shall see in what sense Aristotle "theologises," in what sense he treats of "unmoved" and therefore non-physical things, and in what sense he treats of physical "principles."

3\. The first Book of the Metaphysics (the one we reasonably suppose to be first, from its style and method) starts from an investigation of the words Art and Experience, which we shall meet again in the Ethics, and rises to an examination of the word Wisdom, or σοφία. Wisdom is knowledge of certain causes and principles. The obvious next question is what kinds of causes and principles, but Aristotle thinks he has already answered it implicitly. Sense and experience take note of individual cases. Wisdom rises to the first causes and principles, the most universal, those furthest from casual observation. I must give his own striking words. Through Wonder men have always begun to philosophise. At first they wondered at the difficult things close at hand, then little by little asked about greater things: the changes of the moon, the sun and stars, the origin of the universe. But whoever is puzzled and wonders thinks himself ignorant. So the philosopher is in a way a lover of fables, for fables are made of wonders. Since men philosophised to escape ignorance, they evidently pursued knowledge for its own sake and not for any advantage. The facts support this: this wisdom began to be sought only when the necessities of life and leisure were already supplied. Just as a man is free who exists for his own sake and not another's, so this is the one wholly free knowledge, because it alone exists purely for itself. So its possession may fairly be thought not to belong to man, since human nature is often servile. According to Simonides, "God alone would have this prize," and it is unworthy of us not to seek the knowledge that is proper to him. "If the poets speak truly that the Divine Nature is envious, Simonides must be right, and all over-learned people must be unhappy. But the Divine Nature *cannot* be envious; rather, as the proverb says, 'the poets lie.' No study is more honourable than this, for what is most godlike is most honourable. Now a science may be godlike in two ways: because it is what God has above all others, or because it is itself the knowledge of the Divine. This one is both, for God seems to be a kind of beginning of causes, and God alone or chiefly will possess this knowledge. All other kinds of knowledge are more needful for common purposes, but none is better."

4\. The wise man presented here is not the old Greek sage who could outwit his fellows and found a tyranny. He is not the anxious questioner asking in every direction, "Where is wisdom found?" He is not the Sophist who brings every kind of knowledge to market and sells it to the highest bidder, pledging that it will bring power and its fruits. He is not the Socratic philosopher asking everything he sees for the meaning or truth hidden in it, nor the Platonic philosopher seeking what holds knowledge, society, and nature together. He must be carefully distinguished from, and set against, the man of business or practice (a man highly to be prized in his way). He has a function entirely his own, one that raises him almost to a Divine level and makes him the one fit beholder of what is Divine. And what is the Divine? The answer is that it is *the Cause*: what lies beneath all other causes, is not subject to accident, movement, or the law of growth, and is the original root of all things. Here we have the Aristotelian theology.

5\. But by this definition the theology is *Metaphysics*. It comes after physics in the order of discovery: after because it is implied in physics, and after because it is beyond them. For that very reason it cannot be separated from them, since you cannot know what it is except by its relation to them. I have been careful until now to use the name as little as possible, at least in sketching the Greek inquirers; it is dangerous to anticipate a name. The time will surely come when it is imposed if it is wanted, and a definer of boundaries will appear and say, "This is Morals," "This is Physics," "This is Metaphysics." If he shows he has a right to be heard, we must listen. But we shall understand his exact limits far less well if we have not first let earlier students take their own course and explain themselves. However grateful we may be to a teacher for telling us what to call each thing, we must be careful not to take him as the interpreter of his predecessors. He has a service of his own to render, but it is in the nature of that service that he be an over-strict disciplinarian. He insists that guerilla troops, whose worth lies in their sudden and irregular appearance, follow the rules of regular warfare. He compels men whose order is as strict as his own but wholly different to adopt his signs and divisions, on pain of being treated as disobedient and lawless.

6\. This applies especially to the able review of earlier Greek philosophers in the first book of the Metaphysics. Causes, Aristotle says, are fourfold: (1) the substance of a thing, what constitutes it; (2) its matter, the needful condition of it; (3) the source from which its motion comes; (4) the reason, purpose, or good of its existence. No one can deny the value of this classification for Aristotle's purposes, or that, used with moderation, it can help a student clear his mind about his own aims. But Aristotle believes each school of Greek thinkers followed one of these courses of inquiry and took it as the only and sufficient method. So the Ionic philosophers studied the matter of things, hoping to find a primary element to which all else could be referred. Those who chose fire were led by its effects to speculate on the mystery of Motion. That started a new kind of inquiry, pursued much in the spirit of the inquiries about elements, until Anaxagoras discovered the need for an Intelligence to set physical agents moving. But he turned to this ultimate principle only when other instruments failed him, so the Atomic theory, which explained nature's facts more plausibly than his Homœomeriæ, easily replaced them. Between this theory and the one that made Numbers the first principles of things, Aristotle seems to detect a connection, not well supported by chronology. The doctrine of numbers he takes as the first form of the inquiry into the essence or substance of things, and the archetypal ideas of the Platonists, who regarded numbers as intervening powers between sensible things and pure essences, as the second and higher form. The inquiry about the purpose of things, he imagines, was not pursued distinctly by any class of predecessors but entered confusedly into the speculations of them all.

7\. Now if Socrates was, as I have maintained, the keystone of Greek speculation (an opinion supported by many passages in Aristotle himself), this historical sketch, however ingenious, cannot be correct. In it Socrates is an interloper, rightly mentioned only in a parenthesis as devoting himself chiefly to ethical inquiries, and Plato's intellectual descent is traced, not through him, but to the Ionian and Heraclitan schools. Throughout this treatise Aristotle shows a lack of sympathy with his predecessors. It must have made it impossible for him to understand their complicated thoughts and anxieties, even had he not been set on arranging them, and so he was needlessly irritated with those whose vagrant habits defied arrangement. Yet his hints about other men help us greatly to know him. The Metaphysics are troublesome reading, partly from frequent repetitions and partly from the difficulty of finding a sequence in the books. Still, any student who wants to investigate the questions that have occupied later men should read them. To illustrate my earlier remarks I shall trace a very rough outline of the subjects discussed and record some of the solutions Aristotle gives to the difficulties he raises.

8\. A kind of appendix after the first book proves that causes are not infinite, so that the inquiry earlier philosophers engaged in can be carried on. This same short book contains important remarks on how the search is to be conducted; on the contributions each school may have made; on the advantage a philosopher may draw from attending even to popular notions; on some men's dislike of exact mathematical reasoning and others' insistence on nothing else; and on limiting mathematical accuracy to things without matter. It also clearly announces a principle the student of Aristotle must always keep in mind: the end of theoretical science is truth, of practical science, work. He adds an explanation that further shows how he differs from his master: the practical man has nothing to do with the eternal or the absolute, only with the relative. This book ends with a promise to inquire into the meaning of the word Nature, which the book commonly placed next does not fulfil.

9\. This second book is a collection of doubts or questions to be resolved later. The first is whether it is the business of one science or of several to inquire into all kinds of causes or principles. That question involves the subject of the whole treatise. The province called σοφία seems to include so many different things (matters purely of sense, causes of motion, the nature of Being, the reason and purpose of things) that it is hard to suppose one science dealing with principles that apparently admit neither analogy nor contrast. Second: are the Principles of things the most comprehensive Genera to which individual things can be referred, or the atoms of which they consist? Third, connected with it: is there anything besides individual things? If not, how can they be known, since individual things are infinite and knowledge is of what is one and universal? Fourth: are the principles of perishable and imperishable things the same? Fifth, the great question of all: are Being and Unity the essences of things that are, not distinguishable from them, or must we seek τὸ ὄν and τὸ ἕν as if each had a distinct nature? Sixth: are numbers, bodies, planes, and points substances or not? These are the general controversies we hope to see settled in the following books.

10\. The third book may be taken as an answer to the first question. There is a science that contemplates Existence *as* Existence, and whatever belongs to it in that respect, unlike other sciences, which treat only attributes of particular existences. There are things peculiar to Being as Being, and the philosopher's function is to investigate the truth about them. The dialectician and the sophist resemble the philosopher; Being is the common subject-matter of all three. But they only *discourse* about the subjects that are in a peculiar sense his property. Dialectical power differs from the philosophical in its nature, the sophistical in the intention of the user: dialectic is tentative about what philosophy knows, and sophistry is apparent knowledge that is not real. This passage shows the difference between Platonic and Aristotelian dialectics. For Plato dialectics was *the* human science, the philosopher's science as such, and σοφία in its essence belongs to God. Aristotle finds a difference between dialectics and the highest philosophy, and inevitably: since his dialectic treats only the forms of human thought and deals with knowledge as the powers and means of knowing that we possess, there must be another science about the objects of knowledge as such. But in this science too the objects cease to be objects and become *subjects* for man's contemplation; they become Metaphysics or Ontology. The hint about the *Sophist* here should also be compared with the elaborate portrait of his character and functions in the dialogue between the Eleatic stranger and Theætetus. It connects with the question whether mathematical axioms are matters for the Ontologist or highest philosopher. The answer is yes. The mathematician assumes these axioms; the student of physics sometimes meddles with them, rashly and presumptuously; as first principles they can be known only by the person whose office we are defining. Since the Sophist especially delighted in denying axioms, saying the same thing can be and not be, this is the natural place to settle his pretensions. True to their difference, Aristotle shows him not, as Plato does, as an inventor of counterfeit images of what is, but as one who attributes accidents to accidents instead of to substances. We can hardly imagine two portraits of the same person so correct and so felicitous, yet so thoroughly expressing the manner and principle of their respective artists.

11\. The fourth book is a book of definitions. I can convey its importance only by listing the words defined: Principle (ἀρχή), Cause, Element, Nature, Necessity, Unity, Being, Substance, Sameness, Opposition, First and Last, Power, Quantity, Quality, Relation, Perfection, Limitation, "in respect of which" (*secundum quid*), whose meaning is easily seen in Touchstone's words in *As You Like It*: "In respect that it is of the country it is a good life, but in respect that it is not of the court it is a vile life"; Disposition, Habit, Passion, Privation, Inclusion, Derivation, Part, Whole, the mutilated, Kind, Falsehood, Accident. Some of these explanations are evidently repeated from the *Categories* and the *Analytics*. Some are better understood from the arguments in later books than from any formal definition. Still they are worth reading and reflecting on. The one on "Nature" is perhaps the most important, but the only satisfactory clue to his notions of it is the whole treatise.

12\. The fifth book explains Aristotle's view of the difference between physical and ontological science. They agree in being theoretic, not practical or productive. They differ in that the physical deals with what is capable of change or movement and is embedded in matter, while the primary philosophy deals with the unchangeable and what is separate from matter. Mathematical science lies between: like ontology in the first respect, like physics in the second. If there is such a science as Theology, it must be a part, and the highest part, of the primary Philosophy. The Divine Nature must pre-eminently be outside the circle of composite and movable things. The things physical science treats are subject to accidents. There cannot be a science of accidents, since as such they exclude science altogether. But there may be, and are, principles and causes of things that admit accidents. In seeking these, physical science keeps its formal distinction from the higher science that deals with beings and essences as such. There is another sense of "Being," used especially by the Platonists, that must be distinguished from ours: Being had been confounded with truth, and Not-being with falsehood. Now truth, according to Aristotle, is in the mind, not in things. Affirmation combines, negation separates; falsehood separates what should be combined and combines what should be separate. But the existences we are dealing with are simple or uncompounded. Here again is a capital and vital point of difference between the two philosophies.

13\. The sixth book contains some of the most important distinctions in the whole treatise. Ontology is concerned with Substance. What is to be counted a substance? Are walking, sitting, being in health substances? No: all these imply a subject to which they refer. In this way we get rid of the notion of a substantial good, and of a substantial warmth or whiteness. All these are qualities of some subject, and what that subject is must be sought in each individual that presents itself to our observation.

14\. But does not substance, so considered, necessarily connect itself with body? Here is one of our great puzzles. Some would make the *boundaries* of body substances; some would have substances not cognizable by the senses at all; some would make the One the primary substance and suppose other sets of substances, such as numbers, magnitudes, souls, generated from it. Aristotle's opinion is this. Every subject has, first, ὕλη, which we must translate matter, and second, μορφή, form. The matter of a thing is its necessary condition. But matter is not its essence; something else is implied in it, something it presents or makes manifest. Applying this to the questions that occupied the third class of philosophers in the introductory book, the Platonists and Pythagoreans, this form is the true εἶδος they dreamed of. It is the essential thing in each thing, that by which substance is possible and without which it is inconceivable. But it does not exist apart from each particular subject. It enters into the definition of every subject, and without it the definition would be none; so it must be viewed in that subject and cannot be contemplated as a distinct, peculiar essence. By this rule, all notions of an ideal form of hollowness or of pugnosedness (Aristotle's favourite illustration) are out of the question. These cannot primarily be subjects of a definition; they presuppose something whose properties they are, and only in that can you look for an εἶδος. Every notion of Being as distinct from the particular person or thing that is, falls to the ground. Socrates and the being of Socrates are identical, and the αὐτοέκαστον ("each thing itself") of which Plato talked is nothing but this εἶδος, or form, inherent in the thing.

15\. The way this same principle applies to another class of inquiries, those about the origin of things, needs closer examination.

In any production we find, first, something from which it has been generated; second, something by which it has been generated; third, the result, the thing itself. There are three modes of production: natural, artificial, automatic. In natural productions we see a matter at once; indeed Nature itself, in the largest sense, may be defined as that out of which things are produced. Everything that becomes has a nature, which is another way of saying it has a ὕλη, and that in each thing which might not have been is this ὕλη. The result formed from this matter is some substance: a vegetable, a beast, a man. But what is the producing cause in each case? Clearly something of like kind with the result: a man generates a man. So the resulting thing implies a productive force distinct from the matter it works on, and this is our εἶδος. The combination of εἶδος with ὕλη both produces a substance and constitutes it. Now look at artificial productions. Here too the εἶδος is the producing power, and it is in the soul. The physician's art, the architect's plan, is the εἶδος that produces actual health or an actual house. But here a distinction arises: these productions involve a νόησις and a ποίησις. The νόησις is the perception and inner holding of the form; the ποίησις is the making out of the given matter. The third mode, neither strictly natural nor artificial, works by the action of the thing itself. For instance, a cure may come by applying warmth; a body may become warm by rubbing; this warmth in the body is either itself a portion of health or leads to something like itself that is. Evidently this implies the earlier presence of either nature or an artificer. And this kind of generative influence must combine with another: there must be a productive power and something out of which it is produced. In every case, then, there is an ὕλη and an εἶδος. What is generated is the whole substance, of matter and form. But the form, properly speaking, is not generated. It is reproduced in each particular subject in combination with a certain matter, and becomes a new and peculiar form by that combination. Every production therefore needs a certain form and a certain matter. All the qualities of the produced substance *must* inhere (potentially, not actually) in the substance producing, and *may* belong to the form when they are produced.

16\. It remains to consider how this doctrine bears on the philosophers who busied themselves with a primary element. The inquiries of those who sought the τὸ οὗ ἕνεκα (that for the sake of which) are reserved for another discussion. But first several doubts of the second book must be resolved. First, the meaning of Part and Whole. The most obvious sense of part concerns quantity, but that has nothing to do with our subject. We want the connection of the idea of *Part* with substance. Granting the division of substance into ὕλη and εἶδος, we should say that in a brass statue the brass is *part* of the statue as the complex of form and matter, but not of the statue considered as a Form. Now since Form is the proper subject of a definition (it can be described in itself, while the material cannot), in some cases we necessarily speak of parts as constituting the whole and in others not. We define a circle without reference to its parts, and a syllable by the letters that compose it. The parts of the circle are material parts; the parts of the syllable are formal, logical parts. If you regard a syllable as composed of letters in wax, or of sounds in the air, its divisions become material and fall outside a logical definition. Again, in a material division the whole precedes the part. Logically and formally, the part precedes the whole. If you define the life of an animal, you will describe it by some of its functions; none of its other functions can be performed without sensation, so sensation will be assumed in the existence of the whole animal. This holds equally for matter of the senses (æsthetic) and for matter of the mind (noetic: the figures of mathematics). Generally, then, the question of priority between "part" and "whole" depends on the distinction between matter and form, and cannot be settled if that distinction is disregarded. Aristotle admits, however, the difficulty of defining by form alone, and not by the complex substance of form and matter together. He acknowledges that the attempt to divide matter from substance and treat sensible things as not sensible led to all the Pythagorean and Platonic inventions he so dislikes.

17\. Another question, involving the same philosophers, follows at once. How are substances connected with *kinds*? If there are types after which all sensible things are formed, these types would seem to be universals, and what the senses deal with, particulars. On this view all differences and properties found in the most marked individual of a kind would have to be included in those primitive universal forms; but by logic the opposite is the case. The genus is stripped of the difference that goes to make up the species, and of the properties that go to make up the individual. Your genera can never be types of the individual; by their nature they lack all that characterises him. So the εἶδος, which is the essential in each thing and makes it what it is, must be seen as individualised by the ὕλη it is joined to. Apart from that modification it is only a logical existence, the highest genus to which it is finally referred being pre-eminently what can be contemplated only by and in the mind. This I take to be Aristotle's meaning, and from it follows closely the doctrine with which he winds up this book, applying it to those who dealt mainly with ὕλη. Given any fact or thing, I have no further need to trouble myself about the fact, the ὅτι; the sense, or something like sense, supplies that. When I see a musical man I am not to ask what the musical man is; he is what I behold and nothing else. My business is with the διότι, the cause: why is he this or that? The answer is in the εἶδος, the form or constitution, the ultimate reason of what each thing is. So I get no nearer the cause of things by reducing them to natural elements. The analysis may be physically proper or useful, but it does not lead to what I seek. Whatever is, and whatever I can behold with senses or mind, is not the A or the B of which it is composed but something else. The synthesis of A and B involves the presence of a form or existence that cannot be found in either separately. So find as many primary elements as you will, you have not found an ἀρχή.

18\. Our main business, then, is to find the meaning of this εἶδος and its relation to the ὕλη. The seventh book takes up the subject and carries forward a hint from the last, perhaps the most pregnant in Aristotle's writings and the one with most effect on his whole philosophy. The εἶδος or μορφή is an energy; the ὕλη is a δύναμις, a capacity that needs the action of the energy to produce a result. Οὐσία, as I said, is the synthesis of these. If you leave out the ἐνέργεια you come only to material elements and combinations that do not give you the actual things you are examining. The difficulty is about things that seem to have no ἐνέργεια in themselves, such as a house. Must their substances be something distinct from and external to them? The answer: do you ask whether the material house, the stones and cement, is a substance? Certainly not; you have excluded the notion of substance by the way you put the question. But do you ask whether that house is actually something? Your question assumes it. You cannot define anything without treating it as a substance, however you satisfy yourself about why it is so. So there is something, not distinct from the house but implied in it, which is a form or εἶδος. The test that it exists is your own attempt to define it.

19\. On this principle of an energy and a δύναμις in each substance, he shows how necessary it is in any inquiry after causes to keep three questions in view. By cause do you mean capacity, δύναμις? Moving power? The form? (The last two *may* always be the same, but the inquiry into the constitution of each thing is distinct from the inquiry into its productive force.) Or lastly, do you mean the οὗ ἕνεκα? (This too may be the same question as the last, differently stated: the constitution of each thing may determine its purpose.)

20\. But this use of "capacity" (δύναμις) raises another doubt. Has not every subject a capacity for contraries? Must we not say that every healthy body is potentially sick, and water potentially both wine and vinegar? The answer is that this absence or deprivation of qualities is an accident of those qualities and not itself a quality. For a dead body to become alive it must pass into a certain ὕλη that has the potentiality of life; for vinegar to become wine it must pass into water; nothing similar happens in the opposite case. Finally he applies this principle to the difficulty about unity, which he had often hinted at and on which he had already spoken plainly. If a man is defined as a biped animal, why does each of these, "animal" and "biped," not constitute a separate entity? What, in short, grounds our conception of each thing and person as one? Aristotle suggests that the question is hopeless if put in this form, since each variation of your definition creates a new puzzle. He would rather assume the unity of each thing as a fact or datum of the understanding and account for its being reduced to different constituent elements. This *is* accounted for by the necessary co-presence of matter and form in each thing, matter being merely a potentiality and form an energy. In this way the dream of a *unity* distinct from the individual thing is got rid of, as the dream of a *substance* distinct from each particular thing was before.

21\. The eighth book is still occupied with capacities and energies. Aristotle inquires into the different senses of δύναμις: what we mean when we say a thing can or cannot be. There is a use of this language in geometry that is metaphorical and not to our purpose. We say a thing can or cannot be, meaning that it is or is not. But all δυνάμεις in the proper sense refer to some primary δύναμις, *which is the cause or beginning of a change in some other thing as another thing*. Warmth, for instance, is a δύναμις, equally in what warms and in what is warmed. The δύναμις in the thing warmed refers to the corresponding primary δύναμις in the thing warming, and this necessary implication of what answers to itself in something else is its characteristic nature. To every capacity there corresponds an incapacity, which may mean the absence of a faculty of communication, the absence of a faculty of reception, or merely a negative want, or a positive state involving that want.

22\. Δυνάμεις are divided into rational and irrational: the rational subsist in the reasonable soul, the irrational are merely physical. All art and knowledge are of the first class. A radical distinction between them can be found thus. Warmth, an irrational δύναμις, can only produce warmth; the physician's art can produce either health or sickness. In general, one kind of power can produce contrary changes, the other only one change; and the contrary changes are wrought by the rational powers with and upon the irrational.

23\. This description of δυνάμεις might seem in some points to trench on the notion of energies given in the last book, so Aristotle goes on to distinguish them. The Megarian sect, following their general rule of reducing every idea to that of Being and excluding any distinct notion of production and *becoming*, had identified Power or Capacity with energy.
