Where there is no building, they said, there is no builder. Apply this to arts, says Aristotle, and the man who has studied longest ceases to have the art as soon as he stops exercising it. Apply it to things without reason or life, and nothing is sweet, warm, or cold except at the moment it is tasted or felt. In our minds this is perhaps not very destructive of their proposition, but it was very effective against the Megarians, who had a great horror of the Protagorean doctrine. So a thing may have the capacity of being and not be, and the capacity of not being and be; what it is the capacity of takes place when something is added, which is Energy. Energy is analogous to motion. You cannot predicate motion or energy of things that are not; the moment energy or motion is added, they are. But many things that are not have a possible or potential existence. (As a note: this passage determines as clearly as any the meaning of ἐντελέχεια, the opposite of *potentiality* yet ill translated by "actuality." Εἶδος expresses the substance of each thing at rest, its form or constitution; ἐνέργεια its substance as active and generative; ἐντελέχεια seems the synthesis of the two. Cicero's *effectio* gives its most important side but not the whole.) Yet Energy is not to be confounded with Motion. The difference is not where we might suspect, that motion belongs to the irrational and energy to the rational, for Learning falls under Motion and sight under Energy. The difference is that every motion is incomplete, tending toward an end without including it, while energy has its end in itself and involves no pause or termination. Learning, building, walking all imply a termination. Seeing, thinking, being happy imply none; these are Energies.

24\. On this showing, energy precedes δύναμις in the order of reason and of substance, though not always in the order of time. It precedes in reason because the first of all capacities is the capacity of energising: a man who has the faculty of building has in him the capacity of using his energy in the art of building. In time it is otherwise. The primary energising power precedes, even in time, what receives its impression, Form being older than Matter. But in any particular person or thing, we say its capacity of being that person or thing precedes its being so actually. Yet even then there is always a prior energy presumed in some other thing to which it owes its existence. So we get a principle we shall find put to practical use in the Ethics: the exercise of any particular energy precedes the habitual use of the faculty, and it is by playing the harp that we become harp players. Several important ethical doctrines are developed in this inquiry, but I shall notice them later. One more pregnant notion bears directly on our present subject. Δύναμις was defined as the cause of change in another thing as another thing. But this notion needs a resting-place, unless there is some primary δύναμις presupposed in all others, which is the beginning of motion. This is φύσις, and so we have reached the most complete notion of it we can expect. Energy still precedes and surpasses this first and primary cause of all change.

Aristotle goes on to show how Energy is better and more glorious than δύναμις, for instance because δύναμις is the same for contraries. The capacity of health and sickness is the same; so for stillness and movement, rising and falling. But one of each pair must be good, so the energy that determines which contrary takes effect must be better than the Faculty or Capacity. Two consequences follow. First, Energy is what makes things be evil that have only the possibility of evil in them. Second, in things primary and eternal there is no evil, no fault, no decay; the capacity for evil lies in Nature. Every moral and theological student will feel the importance of these two axioms. Another proposition, widely applied in another direction, is added about *discovery*: it means bringing into Energy things that exist potentially, because Knowing is an Energy.

25\. This book ends with another reference to the relation between truth and being, falsehood and not-being. Truth and falsehood are the accordance or discordance of our judgment with the actual state of things, and three cases fall under notice. First, things always united and inseparable, or always separable and never united. Here the judgment must be uniform: the same will be true in all cases, false in all cases. Second, things that may be separated or united. Here comes the possibility that something is true today and false tomorrow, or true in one aspect and false in another. Third, things perfectly simple, admitting neither division nor combination. To these the words true and false do not apply, only knowledge and ignorance: you either know such things or you do not. About them no mistake or deception is possible, only the presence or absence of knowledge. All sensible objects whose existence you ascertain by touch or sight are of this kind; the lack of touch or sight, not a false opinion, shuts them out from you.

26\. In the ninth book we meet the question of unity again. The name One, he says, is used in four ways: for what is *continuous by nature*, for a *whole*, for an *individual thing*, and for what is *predicated of a whole*. The general sense of the Indivisible is common to all. Unity in any of these senses may be attributed to a substance that is inseparable in place, form, and thought, as well as to an actually indivisible whole. The fundamental notion of unity he takes to be that of a measure of quantities; without such a measure quantity is inconceivable. There may be something actually indivisible, or something indivisible to our senses; an actual unity in form and a supposed unity in matter. Each bears the name because each is used as a measure. He asserts the need for such a measure against the Protagorean notion that man is the measure of all things, which he treats as a silly truism that puts on the form of a paradox and has the effects of a falsehood.

27\. A distinct absolute unity is denied on precisely the same ground as a distinct absolute substance. The One is always *some* one thing or nature. In colours, if you suppose them all to come from white, white is the one; in voices, the elementary vowel; and so in all cases. So the Ionic attempt to find some matter, such as air or fire, to be unity is as unreasonable as the Parmenidean, Pythagorean, and Platonic attempts to give unity itself a formal and separate character.

28\. "The One" is the undivided or indivisible; this is its primary notion, to which all others reduce. "The many" will then mean the divided or divisible, from which, as more cognizable by the senses, the One will be inferred. Next: how are the one and the many opposed? Are not the "many" and the "few" equally opposed, and if so, is unity not merely an element of plurality? This brings in a discussion of the different modes of opposition: contradiction, relation, privation, strict contrariety. Possibly there is some confusion here of different lectures or reports, for in the lengthy explanation we seem to lose sight of the original subject. My readers will have noticed how much the idea of a "law of opposition" in things entered all Greek speculation, so as to seem to many its foundation. Aristotle approaches it from the logical side: the forms of opposition he finds in our minds determine his view of the actual opposition in nature. So his remarks, though apparently irrelevant, throw considerable light on his doctrine of unity. What our understanding needs in order to explain to itself the existence of multitude, he called "the One." In his mind, unity was therefore identical with Singleness.

29\. The next book is mostly a recapitulation of puzzles and solutions already given. It is not to be passed over on that account, for Aristotle's repetitions of himself, or the reports of his different pupils, generally clear away difficulties. Here especially the remarks on the nature and limits of the primary philosophy, and his refutation of Protagoras's two cardinal sophisms, are in many respects fuller than in the third book. I shall notice only his analysis of Motion and his remarks on the Infinite. Motion is neither an energy nor merely a potency; it must be contemplated alternately as each. A lump of brass is potentially a statue; the energy that will make it one is in the sculptor's mind. The motion, the transition from brass to statue, is found neither in the brass nor in the mind; it is what gives the brass's potentiality its meaning and connects it with the energy. In a formula: "Motion is the entelechy (the perfecting power or principle) of the potential as potential." He admits the difficulty of finding an expression for this idea, but shows by examining earlier attempts that his own, however awkward, is the only satisfactory one.

30\. On the Infinite, which had so exercised earlier Greek speculators and had been resorted to as the ultimate solution of so many difficulties, he aims at no precision of language, because by its nature it excludes precision. To fit it into a scheme, or treat it as a helpful definition of nature or the universe, is in his judgment absurd. It can only mark the *ne plus ultra* to which human thought and inquiry can reach, or already have reached. By its very name it excludes the limitations by which alone you can deal with subjects within human cognizance. His opinion here is characteristic of his mind and bears importantly on the history of metaphysics. Scarcely any question interested the Greek mind more than the one at issue between the schools of Pythagoras and Xenophanes: whether it is truer and more reverent to speak of God as the Limit or as the Infinite. Aristotle's concluding remark on the Infinite should be quoted for the incidental light its latter clause throws on his idea of Time, an idea modern students of philosophy need to reflect on. The infinite is not the same in magnitude, motion, and time, as if it were one nature; rather the later is spoken of in terms of the earlier, as motion in terms of the magnitude over which it moves or changes or grows, and time by means of the motion.

31\. Book eleven opens with an attempt to fix the number of causes or first principles. Every sensible substance has a capacity of change, and the changes are four: in substance, quality, quantity, and place. Generation and corruption name the first, growth and decay the second, alteration (ἀλλοίωσις) the third, transference (φορά) the fourth. These are changes into contraries. But contraries themselves do not change; something must undergo the change from one to the other, and this something must be *Matter*. Being does not arise from not-being; being in potency is changed into being in energy, and being in potency is matter. Granting this, there are three ἀρχαί or first principles: the two contraries Form and Privation (στέρησις), and the Matter that passes from one to the other.

32\. But Aristotle says this statement strictly applies only to material things, where the element (στοιχεῖον) and the principle (ἀρχή) are the same. You have heat, the εἶδος; cold, the στέρησις; what has the potency of being either, the ὕλη; and the resulting substance, the flesh, bone, or whatever it may be. But in things apprehended by the mind another idea intrudes. Besides the two opposites, health and sickness, and the matter susceptible of both, you have the health-making art of the physician. The principle, so used, acquires a double meaning that the element does not have: it must be contemplated both as the stationary Form and the moving Power. There must in a sense be four ἀρχαί, though only three elements. The reader will see that these conclusions were partly anticipated, but they need repeating here, for here is the link between Aristotle's Metaphysics and his Theology; this is the road, or one step of the road, by which he reaches the conception of a First Moving Cause. Most of this remarkable book is devoted to unfolding that conception. I can only give the results of an argument that Aristotle clearly felt to be the summing-up of his metaphysical series. There must be an eternal, immovable Substance that is at the same time the source of all movement. Its primary notion is that it is an Energy. Potentiality is excluded from it, for the highest form of Being is incompatible with mere capacity of Being. And since Matter and Potentiality are convertible terms, it must be immaterial. There is no refuge from the notion that all things proceeded from darkness and nothingness except in this belief. Energy being prior to mere potency, eternity must be affirmed of the chaos or night out of which things are supposed to be formed in a different sense from that in which it is affirmed of the Primeval Being.

33\. We must attribute a continual *negative* existence to this potency, but a continual *operative* existence only to the First Cause. We need the one to account for Corruption and Decay, the other to account for actual Existence and Life. Matter is in no sense a cause, either to itself or to anything else, while to a First Cause we necessarily attribute self-causation. Other things impart motion after first receiving it; this must be its own Mover. The next step toward apprehending this Being comes from considering our own intellect and will. There is an object of cognition, something to be known, and an object of volition, something to be desired, and these set intellect and will in us in motion. What appears good to each Understanding or Will actuates it; what *primarily* actuates must be what *is* good. In this we find the union of the faculty of knowledge with what is to be known, and of the faculty of will with what is to be desired. So self-contemplation and self-delight must be the essentials of Deity. By other processes of reasoning he concludes that the Being must be without parts (ἀμερής), without passions (ἀπαθές: the reader will notice the change of gender and will easily believe that τὸ θεῖον is a more common antecedent than ὁ θεός), and subject to no vicissitude (ἀναλλοίωτον).

34\. We then approach the grand question: is there one such cause or many? Nowhere is the difference between Aristotle and his master more remarkable. We have seen how tenderly Plato treated his countrymen's mythology, not from cowardice but because he felt it held a latent truth for which no philosophical abstractions or generalities could substitute. Aristotle, having satisfied himself that the *argument* favours one cause, sweeps away every notion that interferes with it. He considers the gods his country worshipped as derived from certain astrological notions, and merely as setting forth the secondary sensible substances that proceed from the first immaterial Cause.

35\. With equal decision, on this new ground, he denounces the philosophical schemes that had been substituted for the old cosmogonies. He charges them with two common vices: they acknowledged an antithesis and contradiction in things but did not take account of the third element, matter, which is the only explanation of the evil and disorder in the universe; and they substituted many original principles for the one.

36\. In the last two books the doctrines of the Platonists and Pythagoreans (on ideas and numbers) are discussed again at great length and with Aristotle's usual ingenuity. I cannot be expected to go over arguments I have so often mentioned, and I am less anxious to present them fully than to prepare my readers to study them where they occur. But I may remark that the continual renewal of these discussions is important for determining the nature and connection of these treatises, and the character of Aristotle's whole mind and system. Evidently he felt the refutation of these opinions, and the substitution of something else, to be in a manner the business of his life. At all events it was the needed preliminary to his more positive work: while his mind was haunted with these notions, the system he proposed to build, whether physical, metaphysical, or moral, seemed to him to rest on a dubious and infirm foundation. So I regard the metaphysical treatises (whether or not they can be reduced to formal sequence and unity) as having this subject at their centre. His aims are these: to show what ideas are not and what they are; to establish that the εἶδος is not distinct from the particular individual substance, existing apart and connecting it with some higher substance, but is merely its inherent form; to connect the εἶδος, the constituent principle of each thing, with the ἐνέργεια that calls it into existence, and so to give the same satisfactory answer to the two Greek inquiries about the nature of being and the origin of matter; to explain the nature and conditions of the ὕλη, and by depriving it of all intrinsic substantial properties and reducing it to a mere potency, practically to get rid of the old Ionic investigations; and finally to hint at a principle his moral writings fully expound, that the final Cause, the οὗ ἕνεκα, is also connected with the εἶδος and ἐνέργεια, so that the good or purpose of each class of substances is known when we know its nature and proper energy. This is the object he pursues most consistently through all his windings and recapitulations in the books of Metaphysics.

37\. But this object connects on one side with *Logic* and on the other (as the scholiast is so anxious to tell us) with *Theology*. I have not seen my way to adopt Ritter's method of identifying the logical treatises with the metaphysical, a plan inconsistent with the very words of the third book. By such a course, it seems to me, we would lose much insight into the philosopher's habits of mind and the growth of his opinions, which we gain by studying the treatises distinctly while acknowledging their closest connection. I believe Aristotle to be primarily and at heart a logician, who became thoroughly enamoured of the forms of logic and convinced they gave a satisfactory account of the facts of the world, and then gradually worked out in his mind an Ontological system that gave the rationale of those forms and interpreted their relation to different phenomena. Now if it is true, as I have maintained, that the *mathematician* has another set of laws, discovered in the course of his inquiries, from those the *logician* knows, we need not be surprised that Aristotle's arguments against Ideas are so constantly mixed with allusions to the Pythagorean study of Lines or Numbers, or that this study was actually the *base* of the principle he is trying to overthrow.

38\. It is on all accounts a more important inquiry how *Theology* became interwoven with either set of speculations. I think it cannot be denied that the recognition of an absolute Being, an absolute Good, gave life to the whole doctrine of Plato and without it the doctrine is meaningless, while for Aristotle it is merely the crowning result, or at least the necessary postulate, of his philosophy. Consistent with this difference, it was a Being to satisfy the wants of Man that Plato sighed for, and a first Cause of Things that Aristotle did homage to. The first would part with no indication or symbol of the truth that God has held intercourse with men and made himself known to them; the second was content to seek in nature and logic for demonstrations of his attributes and his unity. When we use personal language to describe the God Plato speaks of, we feel it suits best with his feelings and principles, even when, through reverence or ignorance, he refrains from it himself. When we use personal language to describe the Deity of Aristotle, we feel it improper and unsuitable, even if, through deference to ordinary notions or the difficulty of inventing any other, he uses it himself. Theology, then, can have no connection with the ethics of Aristotle.

#### SECTION VI. ARISTOTELIAN PSYCHOLOGY.

1\. The metaphysical treatises shed light on the point we last discussed, and that makes them an important introduction to Aristotle's ethical system. From his Dialectics we might have concluded that he rejected Plato's doctrine of Ideas as a scientific account. It would not follow that he gave up the belief in some ideal of excellence, which runs through all mythologies and has never left the hearts of men. But his conception of God simply as the ground of nature shows at once that, in his mind, acknowledging God's perfection has no connection with a scheme of practical life and conduct. He does not lack sympathy with Plato, or with any philosopher who tried to explain men's dreams on this subject. He simply has not found in himself, and does not recognise in others, the want which every age has tried to express in so many forms. So it becomes an interesting question: what foundation in Aristotle's ethics takes the place of the Theology that is so plainly the foundation of Plato's?

2\. The answer leads us to a very important treatise, the three books on the Soul. It contains more of what has in our day, especially here and in Scotland, been called metaphysics than the works that bear that title. The first book, as usual, examines earlier theories. Aristotle thoroughly disposes of the notions of Democritus, Empedocles and the Pythagoreans, and shows why we can never be satisfied with calling the soul motion, or the principle of motion, or the primary element, or number. In the second book he develops his own doctrine. The soul belongs to the category of entities, so it has a matter and a form. Here, as elsewhere, the matter coincides with its δύναμις (potentiality), and the form is ἐντελέχεια (actuality). The soul is neither separately but the result of both. Sight, for example, requires the energy of vision, the faculty of vision, and in addition an organ, an actual eye. What is true of this sense is true of the whole substance of which it is a part. The soul has its energy and its faculty distinct from the organ through which both appear, yet it requires such an organ. The soul is not a body, but neither is it without a body. In general, what distinguishes a living creature (ζῷον) is that it has a ψυχή (soul).

3\. But not all living creatures have a soul exercising the same δυνάμεις (faculties). All the faculties that can exist in any living creature are these five: receiving nourishment (θρεπτική), sensation (αἰσθητική), motion in place (κινητική), impulse or desire (ὀρεκτική), and intelligence (διανοητική). The nutritive faculty is the lowest and is present in every case, so the soul with this faculty alone may be attributed to vegetables. Wherever any higher faculty is present, all the lower exist too. Under each head there is an interesting discussion of the faculty's character and limits. Under the first, the question is whether the life in each plant must be considered the active or only the passive instrument of its self-sustenance. Under sensation, more complicated points arise, and Aristotle enters into the whole theory, examining each sense in detail. This is the discussion carried on with so much liveliness and depth in one part of the *Theætetus*. The opinion there attributed to Protagoras, which Plato does not deny so far as the doctrine of sensation goes, apart from its moral consequences, is nearly the same as Aristotle's: sensation is neither in the organ nor in the object, but is generated between them, as the effect of the medium through which they communicate. The question of the motive faculty at once involves us in something higher than itself, since movement must depend on impulse, and this is true in all creatures. Sense-appearances do generate impulses or desires, but we must not therefore attribute a governing power to sensation. Rather, the nature of the faculty of impulse determines how these appearances influence it, than that it is determined by them. So each creature seems to have a nature, seen expressly in this faculty of impulse. How, then, does man differ from other creatures? Not, it seems, by lacking this impulsive faculty, nor by its being less truly his nature than it is theirs, but by having the dianoetic faculty to direct it and act with it. The true nature of man consists in the coincidence and joint working of these two. So the soul may be considered as having three portions, distinct logically and not materially: one wholly without reason, one rational, and one that participates in reason.

In this psychological system we find the root of the Aristotelian ethics we were looking for: they begin in Psychology and end in Politics.

#### SECTION VII. THE ARISTOTELIAN ETHICS.

1\. At the opening of the Nicomachean Ethics, long regarded as the fullest statement of his views, Aristotle says that Ethics is an introduction to Politics. So he does not identify the two as Plato does; it is possible, indeed necessary, to treat them separately. In his usual way he looks for the grounds of society in the nature of man. He cannot accept Plato's simple willingness to treat outward necessities and accidents as the *occasions* of society. To him that carries a further step, full of Plato's idealism: the supposition that Society itself involves some higher Unity than any particular society expresses. What seems to him to pervade all things, almost as a law of the universe, and above all to be the secret of human order and government, is a principle of equality and adjustment. The same principle, in another form, drives his ethics. Virtue lies in a mean, and in a sense *is* a mean. Government, which is also a mean, is therefore naturally occupied with sustaining the virtue of individuals, and that virtue is in turn government's chief safeguard. This must be noticed, or the connection between the two spheres, which Aristotle acknowledges as much as his master did, will not be apparent. Many difficulties will also seem insurmountable to a reader who takes the Ethics as a complete system and forgets that, for practical purposes, an educating, directing power is presumed to reside in a governing body whose nature and functions are not yet defined.

2\. But Virtue, the attainment of this mean, is not in Aristotle's view the formal aim of a man's life or of society. Once the notion of an absolute good, introduced by those "dear" and troublesome men the Platonists, is out of the way, there remains one obvious and generally admitted end of all human desires: happiness is emphatically the human end. But if it is human, its definition must be sought in what is peculiarly characteristic of human beings. It cannot lie in powers we share with other creatures, so not chiefly in the nutritive or sensory powers. It must consist in some exercises or energies, since in these the soul or life of every creature shows itself. So it must lie in the energies of our best and highest nature, exercised not at intervals but through a whole life, a life with enough outward prosperity to give them free scope.

3\. But all energies need a direction, and the right direction of its energies is the *virtue* of each class. What, then, is specifically human virtue? It must be in the man. On our psychology, the impulsive faculty is the constitutive faculty of the human soul, though its excellence consists in being subject to the reasoning faculty. Virtue will therefore imply the presence and harmony of both, though it will be found most characteristically in the first. So it must be a habit. But of what kind, and what is its aim, given that an absolute good or ideal is out of the question, and that happiness cannot be the aim, since happiness is exactly what we are now analysing? Aristotle says we are not to worry about scientific accuracy in definitions. Our purpose is practical: we want to form an actual man of a certain character, not a theoretical one.

4\. Practically, then, what we blame as mischief and derangement is in every case excess. There is an excess called timidity and one called foolhardiness; one called prodigality and one called narrowness or avarice. But extremes presuppose a mean, and this is what our habit aims at; virtue generally lies in it. Yet we aim at action, and actions are specific, not general. How, then, do we arrive at specific virtues? Their species are determined by their distinct objects: some habits concern external pleasures, others the passions of the mind. In each case the practical purpose defines the virtue. A general description of the excesses and their corresponding means can be given that really applies in all cases. Still, the excess may differ for each man, according to his actual circumstances and his proneness to one side or the other. Liberality, for instance, is practically a different quality in the rich man and the poor, and one must resist the temptation to profusion, the other to meanness. Virtue is therefore in a mean that is a mean relative to *us*, not one that can be fixed absolutely and invariably by rule.

5\. It follows that this habit presupposes a faculty of choice, or predetermination. Is that the same as willing? Clearly not: willing concerns ends, choice concerns the means to ends. It implies a right end and a right determination of the will toward it. It may be called deliberative desire (and the reader will note how steadily the psychological axiom we mentioned is kept in view). But to what cases does this will or counsel apply, and how far does it depend on us? We do not deliberate about things absolute or eternal, nor about things in the sphere of accident. What remains is whatever is done by us or with our concurrence. Some of these may be taken from our control by actual violence, and this gives rise to questions of casuistry, such as whether a virtuous man will submit to do wrong or to die; each case must be decided on its merits. In ordinary cases the doubt is whether inclination is itself a force upon will and reason. Aristotle disposes of this first by remarking that no reasonable man can confuse an influence on the impulse with a force that prevents its operations; and second by admitting that such influences may produce an incapacity for a particular action, but that this incapacity results from a previous habit which need not have been formed. Habits, then, are in our power; actions not always.

6\. With these foundations settled, he turns to the particular ethical virtues, where his acute and often delicate powers of observation have room to work. The temper of mind Shakspeare has wrought into us does not like to see any character as the mere development of one quality. We dislike hearing of a man as the Magnificent, the Magnanimous, the Modest, the Temperate, or the Just. But this doubtless suited Greek habits. Aristides, Themistocles and Cimon, even if they had no set purpose of realising a particular type of character, fall more readily into certain moulds than the traditional figures of ancient Rome or modern Europe. How a similar tendency was revived at one period in Christian society, and how that revival was tied to a scholastic reverence for Aristotle, we may have to notice later.

7\. Two of these virtues deserve special notice, because they throw light on the whole system and on ethical inquiry generally. The first is Justice. Is it not itself an abstract of all the virtues? We have seen Plato's answer in the *Republic*. Aristotle treats it differently, yet shows how much he felt his master's influence even when rejecting his ideas. In one sense, he says, justice is a complex of virtues: it is the habit that chiefly disposes us to obey the laws, and since laws forbid excesses of every kind and encourage every kind of virtue, justice has respect to them all. But there is such an offence as overreaching, and there must be a specific virtue answering to it, related to the general one. The evil is inequality in matters of property; the virtue is evenness or equality. This implies on each side an excess, a more and a *less*, in reference to given persons. The virtue aims at preserving the right proportion between things and persons and restoring the balance when it is broken. Remove the restriction to property, and justice would seem the very virtue of virtues, so emphatically does it preserve the mean. But that restriction makes it harder to say how far the virtue belongs to the individual and how far to the State. So justice, though more narrowly meant, becomes for Aristotle as for Plato a debatable ground between the two regions. At all events it must be regarded as *the* ethical virtue of a statesman.

8\. The other virtue is temperance. It is opposed to intemperance and involves the general notion of an even mind undisturbed by sensual desires, so it might seem to include self-restraint. But in Aristotle's psychology the soul has a rational, an irrational and a quasi-rational part. The quality that controls the irrational or merely animal nature need not be the same as the one concerning the quasi-rational part, the passions and affections. The name temperance goes to the first, self-restraint to the second. A curious consequence follows: this self-government seems distinct not only from the virtue concerned with sensual desires but even from virtue itself, since virtue has its chief seat in the impulsive nature over which self-restraint is supposed to rule. So after the catalogue of virtues we are puzzled to find a book opening with three moral states to be avoided (vice, lack of self-control, brutishness) and three good states corresponding to them (virtue, self-restraint, and a divine excellence as far above ordinary humanity as brutishness is below it). The result seems inevitable to anyone who reflects on the system: what preserves virtue must somehow be distinct from virtue. But it must also be admitted that it considerably impairs the symmetry of a design otherwise singularly complete.

9\. Before Aristotle touched on this preserving power, which belongs to the purely ethical side of man, he had to explain more distinctly than in his psychology the intellectual faculties to which such important functions are given. He begins by reaffirming that the impulsive and intellectual faculties must co-operate for any good moral act; or, as he puts it more neatly and more in line with his own view of the intellect, what the one affirms or denies the other must pursue or avoid. The soul, he says, may affirm or deny truly in five ways: by art, by science, by practical wisdom, by wisdom, by intuition. Art is what we would call the creative or poetic faculty, in its highest and lowest sense. Science is its converse: it deals with what cannot be otherwise, does not fashion but perceives, and concerns universals, not particulars. Aristotelian science, as we have seen, deals with conclusions, not premises. But some faculty must deal with premises, a tact, intuition or spiritual sense, and this is intuition. Its sphere seems very limited, being bounded on one side by science and on the other by wisdom. Wisdom, we were told in the *Metaphysics*, deals with principles, so it might seem to cover all that is assigned to intuition. But that which affirms things to be so without reasoning is surely distinct from the faculty that searches for causes through long and winding labyrinths. When intuition is said to deal with premises, the first operation is meant; when wisdom is, the second. Practical wisdom, the last of the five, is different yet has something of the others. Like art, it deals with what changes and can be changed, but it is perceptive and discriminating, not productive. So far it resembles science, though its subject-matter is wholly different. It has the quickness of tact that intuition has, but only as the result of practice and experience. It is therefore, like wisdom, a laborious investigating faculty, yet its end is practice, not speculation. The practical, experienced man is thus contrasted with the meditative, speculative one, though wisdom may usefully assist practical wisdom. From this analysis it is evident that practical wisdom is the quality which, combined with right character, meaning a proper condition of the impulsive faculty, produces virtue. The doctrine of Socrates that virtue is practical wisdom (stated somewhat carelessly, since the real Socratic doctrine treats virtue as science, the knowledge of what is absolutely good, prudence being only a guardian faculty that protects the soul, in seeking that knowledge, from the seductions and confusions of sense) is said to be only one side of the truth. Practical wisdom is not virtue, though virtue cannot exist without it.

10\. We have seen that justice occupies, to a certain extent, the same position in Aristotle's system as in Plato's, as a link between morals and politics. But Aristotle could not help seeing that justice, under the conditions he had imposed, explained very imperfectly the connection of human society with the life of the individual. This dry, hard principle of distribution, exchange and rectification could never replace the music of Plato's *Commonwealth*. Reflecting on this, he seems to have concluded that Friendship fills the void, and that Friendship and Justice together make up the social law. So seen, Friendship holds the most important place in an ethics always looking onward to Politics, and it is no wonder he gave it two elaborate books. Anyone acquainted with Greek tradition and mythology knows how much the Greek mind was occupied with the subject. There, as elsewhere, physical and moral thoughts were intertwined, and the same language described the law of sympathy between sky and earth and between man and man. Aristotle takes care to free himself from these ambiguous phrases, which he perhaps lacked the imagination to see as more than metaphors, and fixes on Friendship as one of the essential conditions of our nature to which the very existence of communion must be referred.

11\. On this footing he has no hesitation in setting aside many popular notions of friendship as giving an inadequate view of it. He rejects the doctrine that friendship rests ultimately on utility or pleasure, not with sentimental indignation but as contrary to facts and reason. Such friendships are transitory and depend on accidents, which shows the practical man as much as the philosopher that the essence of friendship is not found in them. The friendship of good men for each other must be the source from which we deduce its nature. Here alone we learn its conditions, or even its possibility, for properly speaking it is possible only if one man can really delight in another and love him as himself. A politician seriously reflecting on the existence of society must feel that a principle is at work among men which can be defined only in these terms; that its imperfect appearances in the world, so far as they are imperfect, make its meaning less intelligible; that if selfishness were absolute it could not exist at all; and that the highest form in which it shows itself is the test of its character. Aristotle states these conclusions again and again with great precision. On their strength he says that the idea of equality or proportion is as discoverable in friendship as in justice; only that in justice the worth of each object comes first and its fitness to us second, while in friendship fitness comes first and worth is subordinate. On the same ground he holds that friendship is seen in its true working not in clubs, societies or partnerships, where men associate for some specific object (though justice is needed there too, as assessor and complement, every kind of society implying a law to regulate it as well as a motive to form it, a principle of government as well as of concord), but in a *polity* of which all these are portions. An inquiry into the different kinds of government thus becomes connected with an inquiry into the law of friendship.

12\. In this passage we see how far Aristotle surpassed Plato in understanding relationships, while falling short of him in everything concerning the absolute. In the relations of father to son, husband to wife, and brother to sister he finds three primary forms of friendship, and the grounds of the three kinds of government to which all others reduce: Monarchy, Aristocracy and Timocracy, whose corruptions are Tyranny, Oligarchy and Democracy. Under each true form friendship and justice exist and sustain each other, but friendship takes its peculiar form from the society. In a monarchy it is the friendship of patronage and reverence. In an aristocracy it has the conjugal model, one party being respected as superior in worth and keeping that respect only while he asserts dominion on that ground. The fraternal type of equality is preserved in all friendship under a timocracy. In the depraved forms friendship is depraved and weak, and in tyranny, which he counts the worst because it corrupts the best, both friendship and justice vanish, subjects being regarded as animals and so incapable of human qualities. With this law of sympathy established as one of the two necessary conditions of human fellowship, and virtue shown to be the necessary condition of friendship, Aristotle goes on to settle many questions of deep practical interest. The most important concern the relation between friendship and self-love. In what sense is friendship a part of self-love, and in what sense opposed to it? Since pleasure and utility have already been separated from friendship, the vulgar notion of self-love must be separated from it too. Still, ordinary language suggests some analogy between the two, and it is hard to get beyond saying that the friend is loved as another self. May the difficulty then be solved thus: self-love may itself be distinguished from all thoughts of profit and loss, and the wise and good man is the true self-lover, the only one who is at one with himself and can enjoy his own company? If so, it would be wrong to seek the ground of friendship in self-love; it would be truer to say they illustrate each other. Only the man capable of friendship will have himself for a friend, and only he who can enjoy and love himself can enjoy and love another. These two books on Friendship are certainly not the least profound of Aristotle's writings, and to the general reader they will be by far the most delightful.

13\. From this subject we pass, in the tenth book, to Pleasure: what it is, how far, as some philosophers teach, it is to be denounced as an evil, and how far it is *a good* or connected with *the good*. Aristotle argues against many prevalent definitions. He shows why it is neither a movement, nor a process of coming into a state, nor the filling up of a want. He thinks the universal longing of mankind sufficient witness that pleasure is something real and worthy in itself, not merely a means to another end, and an examination of the facts leads to the same conclusion. But it also refutes the opinion that pleasure can be made a distinct formal purpose of life. It is the flower or consummation of something else. The exercise of sight and the exercise of hearing each bring their own pleasure after them, but the pleasure is tied to the energy and cannot be severed from it. So to understand what pleasure is, and what the highest pleasures are, you must understand what energies are and what the highest energies are. You cannot refer the last to the first; you must refer the first to the last. The energy most appropriate to each creature brings the pleasure appropriate to it; "the energy of the soul, according to virtue," brings man the highest pleasure. The pleasure an act gives the one who performs it is the test that the act has become habitual to him and that he has acquired the corresponding character. The man who delights in musical energies has become a musician; the man who delights in just acts is a just man.

14\. From this analysis of pleasure the step is easy to reconsidering happiness, and so to a brief review of the whole treatise. Happiness he has found to be the end of man, and to *consist in* the use of his highest energies, not, like pleasure, merely to result from it. What, then, is the highest happiness? It is that of the contemplative man. If we can imagine the life of the gods, it is absurd to attribute to them justice, which concerns contracts and conventions, or temperance, which implies temptations they cannot suffer, and so with most of the acts that preserve the mean for man. We must believe their life to consist in contemplation. But those who can attain this celestial life need early discipline and education. They must be trained to avoid the extremes in which evil lies and to practise the virtues which are the conditions of, and preparation for, contemplative happiness, though it transcends them. For the rest there must be a discipline to cultivate whatever capacities they have, or, if they resist, to coerce and punish them. Here is the field for the science of Politics. Aristotle says the Sophists had reduced that science to a mere teaching of how to talk and argue, but its foundations lie in ethical knowledge and ethical practice, and without them it is worthless and rotten. This is the introduction to the book on Politics.

#### SECTION VIII. POLITICS OF ARISTOTLE.

1\. We can give our readers only a few hints for studying this treatise. Valuable and interesting as it is, it can never stand to Aristotle's other writings as the Platonic *Republic* stands to Plato's dialogues. It is certainly closely connected with his Ethics and his Ontology, as our earlier remarks show, but it is connected with them as a set of results and deductions, not as a principal and fundamental part of his design. The doctrine set out so carefully in the Metaphysics, that every substance presupposes a ruling, active power and a submissive, receptive faculty or matter, reappears here in the most obvious outward facts. The relation of male and female is taken as the first hint of society and as containing its principle. The fellowship implied in that relation involves another idea, rule and subjection, which finds its complete expression in the relation of master and slave. Compare these two relations with the relation within each man, explained in the Psychology and illustrated in the Ethics, between the reasoning power, the faculty that shares in reason, and the mere animal nature. You feel at once that they explain each other and show the condition under which society is meant to exist. Where reason is developed and its authority acknowledged, where the faculty that shares in reason is in fellowship with it and subordinate to it, and where the animal nature is controlled, there you have the true condition of the individual man and the true condition of society. There the relations of husband and wife, and of master and servant, will be kept; they will not be arbitrary but lawful and orderly.

2\. In such a state of things only a polity is possible. Since this is the only true condition of each man, a political state is evidently his only proper and natural condition, and every other is anomalous. The constituents of a polity are found in a household, but a house is not therefore a miniature city, and a city is not merely a collection of households. Each has its own nature and laws. Yet each alike consists in human relations, finds in them the end of its existence, and must regard all means and instruments in reference to them. Economy is not primarily the management of the household's goods or property; that is secondary and accidental. It is mainly the right ordering of the household itself. The slave is the link between the two branches of economy: he must be considered an instrument, and yet treated as a man. A polity can be composed only of freemen, as much because only a freeman understands how to obey as because only he understands how to govern.

3\. Almost necessarily it follows from this view, first, that Aristotle should heed the Platonic idea of unity as little in his Politics as in his Metaphysics. He should utterly abhor any attempt to embody that idea by abolishing distinct relationships, since in his opinion these are the very foundation of society. He should count as good all forms of government that rest on some actual relation, and as evil all that have become in any sense arbitrary. He should therefore acknowledge, much as Plato did, three true forms and three departures from them. He should look on the democratic departure, the attempt to set up a society in which all govern, with no more complacency than the others. And at the same time he should take as his ideal the form from which democracy is a deviation, the one that makes all freemen eligible to govern, though not necessarily sharing in government. These seem to us the main principles of the book. Once they are understood, its occasional difficulties and contradictions will be less puzzling, its place in relation to the rest of the philosophy will be felt, and its value as a key to the political science of modern as well as ancient times will be appreciated.

### DIVISION IV.—THE LATER SECTS.

#### SECTION I. GREECE AFTER THE TIME OF ALEXANDER.

1\. Readers may be surprised that so much of this sketch has gone to Greek philosophy while nothing has yet been said of the schools we usually take to be its great representatives. The Epicureans, the Stoics and the Academics are constantly called *the* Greek schools, and many young Englishmen grow up thinking that the thoughts that made Greece wonderful are found in them in their highest form. That impression sits strangely with the facts. What did the teachers of these schools leave by which we can judge them? Of Epicurus we have three letters, preserved by Diogenes Laertius; of Zeno, nothing; of Cleanthes, one hymn to Jupiter; of the Academics, only traditions. I have not discussed the three books of Aristotle's Rhetoric, because among his many books I had to choose those bearing most directly on my subject. But each of those books, and even his short notes on poetry, contains five times as much on moral and metaphysical philosophy as all the Greek remains of the later schools put together (I limit this to the time before Christ). Weigh the words instead of measuring them, and I believe the disproportion would be far greater.

2\. It cannot be answered that Thales, Pythagoras and Socrates wrote nothing and yet did more than most men to awaken the energies that produce books. Epicurus, Zeno and their followers belonged to an age of books and were strictly makers of books; each of them may have written as much as Aristotle. The dialogue, as an exchange of feelings with disciples, can never have been characteristic of them. They were teachers and lecturers who laid down maxims and laws, which their disciples repeated, modified and argued for.

3\. This partly explains and justifies the place they have been given among their countrymen. We have heard of seekers of wisdom, men steadily working at problems. These men had finished their search and solved their problems. They had settled what could and could not be known; they could set down their results in definite, manageable propositions; they had a settled doctrine that could be handed on. So they deserve the name they have been given: they are *the* Greek *sects*. To call them the Greek *philosophers* is absurd, at least if philosophy means what Socrates, or even Aristotle, meant by it. But they did claim one set of conclusions as their own, with fixed theses and formulas that could be learned by heart. They could give the Greeks of their ages what those Greeks wanted, namely topics for disputation; and they could give another and nobler race suggestions that *it* could mould into something like a satisfaction for the cravings of its energetic minds.

4\. The great Alexandrian period followed the republican period: the age when Greeks showed they could subdue barbarians gave way to the age when they defended their freedom against barbarians, or abused it in quarrels among themselves. Aristotle I have taken as the representative of the earlier time. He surveyed and mapped all the provinces of human thought, reproduced his predecessors' inquiries and recast them in his own moulds, and above all gave the wise man and the practical man two quite distinct spheres of activity, though spheres in which each might help or harm the other. To a great extent, then, Aristotle declared the search for wisdom at an end. He left his disciples with the impression that the whole scheme of the universe could be brought under the forms of the human understanding. Much in his teaching counteracted this. The practical man had a vast range for governing his own mind and keeping or improving society, and the divine "Theorist" might hope for a field that was almost or quite inexhaustible. As long as Alexander's age lasted, both would draw strength and hope from the changing state of the world, its newly discovered treasures, and the prospect of Greek wisdom standing at its head.

5\. That dream passed, and some perhaps felt, while it lasted, how brief it would be. An age of intrigue followed, in which all great principles were lost sight of. It proved that the elements of Greek society were absolutely unsociable, and that restoring the older freedom was as hopeless as keeping an empire united. Changes so sudden must have left a sense of weariness and exhaustion in the most active and feverish nation that ever existed, and the speculations of its wise men were sure to reflect that weariness and hopelessness.

6\. I have spoken of scepticism as a Greek characteristic. Far from blaming philosophy for it, I have supposed that philosophy was a great protest against it: a Greek who learned that there was something he could not create had advanced some way toward reverence and belief. But another kind of scepticism was possible, which in some sense may be called the fruit of philosophy. A man might say: "We have been seeking a long time; what have we found? Have we any certain determinations? Aristotle says we have. Socrates and Plato seem to say the opposite, and they are perhaps as good authorities as he. With so many different notions circulating among us for two or three hundred years, who can be confident that any one deserves more respect than another? The wise man has some function, undoubtedly, and we Greeks are still to be the wise people of the earth. But perhaps his function is not to determine anything. May it not be rather to tell us how those should behave who, as Socrates said, know that they know nothing?"

7\. Pyrrho has always been taken as the representative of this state of mind. He is said to have served in Alexander's army and talked with the Indian Gymnosophists, and to have concluded that nothing is noble or base, just or unjust; that nothing truly is; that men do everything by custom and law; and that each thing is no more this than that. Being naturally nervous, he is said to have cultivated, with great success, ἀταραξία (freedom from disturbance), so that he would not step off the road to avoid being run down by a carriage or bitten by a dog. He is said to have lived ninety years, to have been made high priest by his own citizens, who for his sake excused philosophers from taxes, and to have been given citizenship by the Athenians.

8\. Apart from the reports of his conduct, which are only jokes, and not very clever ones, about his efforts after quietness, the outline of this story is quite credible. A Greek meeting an absorbed Brahmin would likely admire his apparent freedom from outward disquiet, and at the same time grow more doubtful about the importance or reality of the questions that had interested his own people. The talk about good and base is the ordinary language of sceptical despair. Since such despair is compatible with believing anything possible because nothing is true, it could not disqualify a man from being a priest. It was not unnatural that the city which in less degenerate days gave Socrates the hemlock should give Pyrrho its highest rewards for stating in words what most Athenians would at once have recognised as their own inward persuasion. Whether Pyrrho's personal history is authentic or fictitious, in substance it is the history of thousands in that time.

9\. But what in this universal scepticism corresponds to the character I have given the *sects*? In one sense Pyrrhonism lay at the root of all of them; in another they were the reaction against it. All of them implied a despair of discovery, of *philosophy* in its old sense, and a belief that the wise man's main object is freedom from the disturbances and distresses of ordinary men. But men who have fallen into doubt through exhaustion soon find doubt itself very *exhausting*. They crave distinct, positive decisions: if possible novel ones, better than any given before, which make them feel superior to those earlier toilers who affirmed little after all their travail; decisions that embody the results of much thinking without requiring the effort of it; but at all events *decisions*, easily set forth and argued for, and usable against any old or new opinion opposed to them.

#### SECTION II. EPICURUS.

::: {.center}
[born b.c. 342, died b.c. 270.]{.smallcaps}
:::

1\. [Epicurus]{.smallcaps}, we are told, liked hearing anecdotes about the indifference and apathy of Pyrrho, and wanted to imitate him in those qualities. But Epicurus was no doubter; he was the most imperious of dogmatists. No one ever had such complete faith in his own conclusions, or so heartily rejected every other conclusion as absurd, even impossible. Without this perfect satisfaction in his own judgment he would have missed his main object. But anyone who sets that object before him is fairly sure to gain such self-confidence. A man must be in a peculiar state of mind to believe that the universe and everything in it exists only to tell him how to be comfortable. Once he believes it, it will be wonderful if he ever hears any sound that is not an echo of his demand or a piece of an answer to it. "Do you deny that all men like pleasure and dislike pain?" is his kind of inquiry. It means simply, "Do you deny that all men like what they like and dislike what they dislike?" — and it leaves an opponent considerably perplexed.

2\. Epicurus liked to boast that he had made his own philosophy, that he was "self-taught." A really original thinker seldom says this; he knows that what he has learnt is his own, and is glad to name his teachers. Epicurus may honestly have read very little and worked out his conclusions in his own mind, but he was a copyist nonetheless, as much as almost any man. Aristotle had shown that if an absolute good is not the end of practical life, happiness must be. Epicurus could say no more than that; he could only look for a new criterion of happiness, since Aristotle's definition — an energy of the soul, exercised in conformity with certain existing relations and implying a body politic — was bound to be unsatisfactory to him. In that search he had no resource but the old sophistical dogma that everything must be referred to the standard of our sensations. He may have refined on it, because he had formulas about sensations from Plato and Aristotle, and because he lived in an age less active in thought but far better versed in everything that serves mere gratification, bodily or mental. It has been much debated, and, I think, needlessly, whether by making sensation his standard he meant to encourage what is formally called sensuality. The ancient testimonies about his character vary. The most recent criticism seems inclined to return to the popular opinion, rightly rejecting the fanatical praises of some French and English writers of the last century. On the whole I am inclined to think Epicurus was an apathetic, decorous, formal man who could cultivate a measured, even habit of mind without great difficulty. He may now and then have indulged in sensual pleasures to prove he thought them lawful, but he generally preferred, as a matter of taste, the exercises of the intellect to the more violent forms of self-indulgence. That life seems to me most consistent with his opinions, since the aim of his philosophy was plainly to avoid commotion and make life's stream flow as easily and uninterruptedly as possible.

3\. To that end it was advisable to avoid the pursuit of wealth and honours, and better to abstain from very vehement enjoyments; but it was absolutely necessary to be rid of all superstitious fears. How these fears arise was therefore an important question for the founder, and the answer led him further than he first meant to go. Naturally he cared only for moral questions, those about the management of human life. He had no real concern with dialectics, Platonic or Aristotelian: what had he to do with a science that distinguishes false from true in things or words? He took even less interest in physics, since the fixed order of nature is a painful weight on a man who wants to arrange a scheme for himself and disbelieves in any actual order and government. Theology, so closely mixed with both in the earlier systems, he would have banished still more resolutely. Yet all these subjects had to pass under his review, because conclusions drawn from them disturb man's serenity and give him bad dreams. A study answering to logic can explain how opinions originate and why some must be called false and some true. A man has sensations, and images are presented to him from outside. The sensations are to be trusted, and so are the representations; error arises only when some power forms a judgment from them. Such judgments are corrected by returning to the sensations, the only ultimate standards. Here again the originality of Epicurus lies wholly in what he omits.

4\. Plato and Aristotle, each in his own way, had treated the relation between the senses and the visible representations set before them with the profoundest skill and discrimination. Epicurus only had to use such fragments from Plato's intuitive observations or Aristotle's rigid analysis as were consistent with rejecting their principle that there is another and surer standard than the senses. In physics he was still a copyist. As he adopted Democritus's theory simply because it was the most comfortable to hold, so, as anyone who studies the rest of his philosophy can see, the many changes he made in it were guided simply by a wish to be rid of some distressing fact that interfered with his moral speculations and made his scheme of life less practicable. The idea of mysterious powers in nature had always haunted the Greek mind, as the speculations of the philosophers bore witness no less than the fables of the mythologists. Connected with it was another thought, still more oppressive to Epicurus and still more damaging to the calm and apathy of the true sage: that these powers were organised, that nature showed vestiges of a scheme, something answering to man's own powers of art and contrivance while also controlling them. From these painful impressions there was one joyful refuge. The notion that the world is composed of atoms which met in empty space and united into the light forms or heavy masses our senses know at once freed physics from its connection with theology.

5\. And since one product of these invisible atoms was what had been called the soul of man, a new light dawned on the moral system from this natural philosophy: a new confirmation of the great principle that man is a machine which, like any other, can be regulated and adjusted to produce desirable results.

6\. There was one fault in Democritus's doctrine. His atoms descended in a straight line, which savoured too much of determinate purpose and fixed law. Suppose them to swerve a little from the line — a very little — and the charm is broken; better still, you have a way of accounting for choice and freedom in man. If Epicurus anticipated an actual physical discovery in this speculation, it is fresh evidence that the divinity he supposed, one taking no interest in human thoughts or designs, does sometimes shape them into a strange resemblance of truth when they are shaping themselves into the most grotesque falsehoods. On the whole it seems unnecessary to attribute to the founder any deep and malignant design of undermining human belief or conduct. The worst that can be said is that he exactly caught the impression of a wretched, emasculated age; the best is that he showed some skill in combining the notions of earlier philosophers into the only scheme that could seem plausible or possible to the men of such an age. One thing should be noticed: if Epicurism is ultimately destructive of moral habits, it is not to these but to science that it directly sets itself in opposition. The Epicurean is essentially the unscientific man — more correctly, the hater of science. That is the more striking because in modern days, when physical science has established itself by another agency than theirs, disciples of this school have sometimes affected to patronise it and even boasted of being its exclusive promoters. Perhaps we shall find, when we come to speak of them, that they have departed more in appearance than in reality from the fundamental principle of their sect.

#### SECTION III. STOICISM.

1\. The second great effort against the Pyrrhonism of this age is the Stoic philosophy. As with the last system, we must try to detach ourselves from Roman associations and portrayals. Doing so entirely is impossible, since these philosophies hardly found their meaning until living Roman minds adopted them. Still, it matters to consider the statue as it came from Greek artists, who could no longer give life to their works, before the Italian Pygmalion embraced it. The sense of intercourse between men and a divine race above them had worked deeply into the Greek character. It had given credence to many fables, some beautiful and some impure, and it sustained every polity and system of laws. We can imagine, then, what its disappearance did. A Greek could not feel bound to his fellow-creatures when he felt cut off from every other kind of being. But this isolation affected minds differently. It drove the Epicurean to ask how he might make a world in which to live comfortably, free of visions of past and future and of dread of powers who no longer stirred any sympathy in him. It drove Zeno to ask whether a man may find enough in himself to satisfy him, however unfriendly everything beyond him may be. This was no new problem for the practical man or the theorist. The Greek sages had met it again and again, tried to solve it, and thoroughly examined the conditions under which it could and could not be solved. So here, as before, we can expect no originality of conception. The interest lies in the complete desolation of heart that led the philosophers of the Porch to try the inquiry once more. The works attributed to Zeno show clearly what was working in his mind. Among other tasks he undertook to answer Plato's *Republic*. The truth that man is a political being pervades that book, and it must have troubled him, and he felt it had to be got rid of before he could assert a man's solitary dignity. He seems to have lived by his principle with consistency and steadiness, showing a real indifference to outward circumstances and, with less dogmatic affectation than many, a creditable independence and uprightness. Zeno taught at Athens. He was not accused of corrupting the youth or bringing in new demons; he received a golden crown and was buried publicly in the Ceramicus. Athens may have sympathised less with his severity than with Pyrrho's indifference, but it could do justice to the good qualities of any man it could understand. Socrates was neither sceptic nor dogmatist; they did not know where to have him, and it was safer to get rid of him.

2\. It is pleasant to meet a hardy, energetic, free man in this dreary, formal time, and *Cleanthes*, Zeno's disciple and successor, seems to deserve that description.

He began life as a boxer and came to Athens with five drachmas. He drew water by night so that he could attend Zeno's lectures by day. He was brought before the Dicastery to explain his healthy appearance when he had no obvious means of living, and was acquitted on the testimony of the gardeners he worked for. He refused the ten minæ the Areopagus offered him as a reward for this discovery. He was reckoned a very slow, toilsome man, and patiently endured being called "The ass," taking it as a compliment that he alone could bear the burden Zeno put on him. Such a man would stand out in any age, and especially among a race of clever talkers, impatient of rough toil and ambitious above all for a name for quickness and wit.

His biographer says Cleanthes wrote many beautiful books. The list of titles makes us glad they are lost, since they might have spoiled our impression of him as a living being and reduced him to a mere discourser on Sensation, Time, Art. He survives truly in his Hymn to Jupiter. It is the only surviving work connecting early Stoicism with the higher, nobler form it took, after its Roman impregnation, in the Life and Meditations of Marcus Aurelius.

3\. The "slow" Cleanthes had far less favour in the Porch than his rapid pupil Chrysippus. "Give me your doctrines and I will find the demonstrations," Chrysippus told the humble burden-bearer. His disciples said reverently, "If the gods have a science of dialectics among them it must be that of Chrysippus." He did not disagree with them. An anxious parent asked, "To what instructor shall I commit my son?" and he answered, "To me; if there were a better philosopher to be found, should not I go to him?"

Such a man was not likely to write a hymn to Jupiter, but he was the man to perfect the Stoical system. Zeno's professed followers had differed widely. Ariston scorned dialectics and in some conclusions seems to have approached Epicurus. Chrysippus ended these heresies and gave the school a definite form and culture. It may be worth showing briefly how the first bare conception of a man striving to live apart from his time and surroundings grew into a compact and tolerably consistent theory of the universe.

4\. The Stoics could hardly invent a division of studies different from the long-sanctioned one, and they extended their theory into Morals, Logic, and Physics. But they introduced a novelty of expression, speaking of *Virtue* as ethical, logical, and physical. These were not three kinds of virtue but three parts composing it: logic was the shell of the egg, ethics the white, physics the yolk. Much can be learnt from this. First, virtue, a state of mind or character in the individual, is all the Stoic can conceive. On it every idea of truth, and of outward tangible forms, depends. This opinion grew easily out of Aristotle's doctrine, yet in some respects it looks less like that doctrine than like Plato's. In the doctrine of Socrates, developed by Plato, virtue is always conformity to a standard outside the man, a participation in the absolutely good, and inseparable from possessing the absolutely true. Aristotle rejected this, trying to place practical morality in one department and the study of Being in another. In his system virtue and happiness gained a substantive, independent character that Socrates and Plato could never have given them. But this separation of Ethics from Ontology also let him follow his natural bent and call the contemplative life the highest, the life peculiar to the philosopher. The Stoic carried his conception of virtue further, cutting it off from any dream of an absolute good. He rejected Aristotle's praise of Contemplation with indignation, and seemed to return to the older Socratic language by describing virtue as a conflict with appearances and deceptions. But he took only the negative side of the Socratic doctrine. His virtue had nothing to converse with or behold but itself. The obstacles and temptations it had to overcome were not those that dim human vision and keep it from its proper object, but only those that make it less conscious of its own independence and glory. The Stoical ethics, then, drew all that was genuine and vital from Socrates' language about the slavery of the undisciplined, sensual spirit. They also borrowed and misapplied many of his phrases about virtue and science. In so far as they were of native manufacture, they were only pithy maxims of conduct, wire-drawn and minute, descending into the lowest details and frivolities. They tended to emasculate character under pretence of elevating it, and deserved the censure that they were fitter for nurses than for philosophers. This was the white of the egg.

5\. That Dialectics should be only the shell shows how little of the real spirit of Socrates survived in this feeble imitation. The language that made science almost identical with virtue meant, in the Stoics' mouths, only that a virtuous man has good taste and a shrewd sense of what to accept and what to reject. They did not recognise truth as reality, or falsehood as a positive opposite to truth. So they had no use for Dialectics in the Platonic sense, the science of distinguishing truth from falsehood. All they understood was that what is desirable is preferred by some and not by others. They therefore asked what in us determines the fitness or unfitness of things, and how we know when things give us a right result and when a wrong one. In all essentials their conclusions were the same as the Epicureans': neither believed in any standard besides sense, or in any difference between the real and the apparent. But their moral schemes differed, so the Stoics were unwilling to admit the likeness. They wanted to persuade themselves, and to some extent honestly could, that those who aimed at virtue would not have so fluctuating a measure of good as those who aimed only at pleasurable results. So the Stoics sought a science, though they could not reach it. In the attempt they invented many acute verbal distinctions which, mixed with some grammar and rhetoric, made up their dialectical virtue, as the rules of behaviour just mentioned made up their moral virtue. It was a fitting comparison to a shell that holds no nourishment and is so fragile that it gives easy passage to the yolk within.

6\. Readers may be surprised that the essential part of the Stoical egg, which as a whole is the type of Virtue, should be the physical part. But this fits both the origin and the later development of Stoicism. The Epicurean undervalued physics because it spoke of something fixed and pre-ordained, inconsistent with the adjustment to circumstances and accidents that he counted his chief good. The Stoic wished to rise above circumstances and take a firm, independent position, and so naturally delighted in contemplating an undeviating system. The perception of any real law and standard for man had left both alike. But some people can never lose their deep sense that such a law is necessary. In despair of finding it, they take refuge in its closest type and counterpart, the sequence of nature's operations. This feeling always brings with it reverence for fate or necessity, and a proud, voluntary submission to its rule. Such a Fate became the Stoic's god, strictly his only god. But he saw it imaged in the movements of the universe, and at times he felt the need of something more real and more connected with himself than an abstraction. So the World became the living form in which he contemplated the object of his worship. He also found it expedient, for strengthening some of his moral habits and in keeping with some of his maxims, not to reject established opinions. So he easily persuaded himself to adopt Aristotle's view that the old legends did represent processes in the material world. They could then be recognised and defended without violating his philosophical dignity. Once theology was identified with physics, it is no surprise that physics should be treated as the most inward and sacred part of morality.

#### SECTION IV. THE ACADEMIC.

1\. One sect remains to complete our picture of Greek philosophy in its decline: the Academic. We have seen that Plato's countrymen had lost the very power of conceiving what I have called the central principle of his philosophy. His language about Being was a dream to them. They could not even follow Aristotle's elaborate arguments against it, and Aristotle's own views about Form and Matter in each substance had become almost as unintelligible as his master's deeper thoughts. We can imagine what this did to the schools that bore their names. The Peripatetic, who worshipped Aristotle's name, could still expound his Physics, his Logic, part of his Metaphysics, and whatever of his Ethics could be separated from the Politics. But the Academics found the incomprehensible part of their master's creed running through all his works. It could be no more detached from the *Phædrus* and the *Phædon* than from the *Republic*.

2\. They had one circumstance in their favour. For reasons I have already considered at sufficient length, Plato develops his great principle in dialogues where two propositions seem set up to knock each other down. What could be more natural than to think he meant to hold men's judgments in perpetual balance, and to cultivate a habit of endless scepticism? The conclusion was plausible. Yet Plato's writings and his whole purpose so plainly refuted it, and overthrowing scepticism seemed so clearly the object of his life, that it was a long time before the notion took hold. The Academy seems to have gone through many changes, and what they were is much disputed. But Cicero's language leaves little doubt that it once asserted certain dogmatic propositions as Plato's doctrine, and that it passed by slow degrees into the purely sceptical society from which he derived, or fancied he derived, his own opinions.

3\. The result is curious but not inexplicable. Scepticism, as I have said, was the foundation of both the other sects, though they tried to break loose from it. The Academic submitted to the spirit of the times and embodied it most consistently in his "no views." He talked eloquently on every subject and carefully avoided concluding anything. He could not say what was right, but he was satisfied that both Epicurean and Stoic were wrong, and that was enough for him. Arguing was his vocation, though it left room for flights and flourishes of rhetoric when the occasion served. In fact the disciples of the philosopher who wrote the *Gorgias* and the *Phædrus* became nothing but teachers of how to be Sophists and Rhetoricians. Such were the dregs of Greek philosophy, from which one may guess how rich its flavour and how full its body must have been in its prime.

## CHAPTER VII. ROMAN PHILOSOPHY.

### SECTION I. ROMAN HISTORY AND MYTHOLOGY.

1\. The ballads or songs thought to underlie one part of Roman history will surely have mentioned many divine persons. But they will surely not have placed at the center a divine counsellor, a God of wisdom. That is not the leading trait of the Roman Jove, nor of any of the lesser demigods. They are lawgivers, rulers, preservers of boundaries, champions of right, avengers of wrong. It could never have occurred to a Latin mind to revere them chiefly, or perhaps at all, for their wit and subtlety.

2\. The heroes of early Roman history and tradition differ in the same way from the Greek hero. There is no Odysseus among them.
